[Page 1]

# Adaptive Bayesian Regression on Data with Low Intrinsic Dimensionality

Tao Tang 1 , Nan Wu 3 , Xiuyuan Cheng 1 , and David Dunson 1,2

1 Department of Mathematics, Duke University

2 Department of Statistical Science, Duke University

3 Department of Mathematical Sciences, The University of Texas at Dallas

# Abstract

We study how the posterior contraction rate under a Gaussian process (GP) prior depends on the intrinsic dimension of the predictors and the smoothness of the regression function. An open question is whether a generic GP prior that does not incorporate knowledge of the intrinsic lower-dimensional structure of the predictors can attain an adaptive rate for a broad class of such structures. We show that this is indeed the case, establishing conditions under which the posterior contraction rates become adaptive to the intrinsic dimension in terms of the covering number of the data domain (the Minkowski dimension) and prove the nonparametric posterior contraction rate, up to a logarithmic factor. When the domain is a compact manifold, we prove the RKHS approximation to intrinsically defined Ho¨lder functions on the manifold of any order of smoothness by a novel analysis, leading to the optimal adaptive posterior contraction rate. We propose an empirical Bayes prior on the kernel bandwidth using kernel affinity and k -nearest neighbor statistics, bypassing explicit estimation of the intrinsic dimension. The efficiency of the proposed Bayesian regression approach is demonstrated in various numerical experiments.

# 1 Introduction

Our interest is in nonparametric regression methodology that can adapt to the intrinsic lower-dimensional structure in the predictors to address the curse of dimensionality. For concreteness, we focus on Bayesian Gaussian process (GP) regression, though our theoretical developments have broader ramifications. GP regression is extremely popular in many application areas due to the combination of simplicity, computational tractability, ease of incorporating prior information and flexibility. We consider the following model:

$$
Y _ { i } = f ^ { * } ( X _ { i } ) + w _ { i } , \ \ w _ { i } \sim \mathcal { N } ( 0 , \sigma ^ { 2 } ) , \ \ i = 1 , \cdots , n ,
$$

where X i ∈ X ⊂ R D , X is the data domain, f ∗ : X → R is the true regression function that generated the data, and w i is a residual error. We introduce the notation f to denote the inferred regression function. We choose a GP prior for f and ideally would like the resulting posterior for f to concentrate near f ∗ . We assume σ 2 is known and fixed for simplicity of exposition, and we discuss possible extensions in Section 6 .

It is well known that nonparametric regression is subject to a curse-of-dimensionality problem depending on the number of predictors D . Given n independent observations on an s times differentiable f ∗ on R D , the minimax nonparametric estimation rate of f ∗ is n − s/ (2 s + D ) [ Sto82 ]. As D is commonly large in modern applications, huge sample sizes may be needed to obtain sufficiently accurate estimates of f ∗ unless some other structure can be imposed to reduce dimensionality. For example, suppose the predictor domain X has intrinsic dimensionality ϱ ≪ D in a sense we will clarify later. A natural question then arises whether nonparametric estimators of f ∗ can adapt to the (typically unknown) intrinsic structure of the data and address the curse-of-dimensionality by achieving the estimation rate of n − s/ (2 s + ϱ ) . The focus of this paper is to develop a Bayesian nonparametric regression estimator that is adaptive to the intrinsic structure in X without requiring prior knowledge of the structure or its dimensionality.

Intrinsic dimensionality in data analysis has been extensively studied in various contexts. For nonparametric regression and classification, a common notion of lower-dimensional structure is sparsity, assuming that a small subset of the features impact the response [LW08, JT21, YT15]. Instead, our focus is on the dimensionality of the feature space X itself. In this context, a popular assumption is that X corresponds to a smooth manifold M⊂ R D [BL07, SN06, CW13, YZ08, YZ09]. In this work, we consider a broader concept of low-dimensionality defined by the covering number, which includes the Riemannian manifold as a special case. While also obtaining general results on posterior contraction rates depending on the covering number, we show a minimax-optimal adaptive rate n -s/ (2 s + d ) (up to a log factor) in the case of a d -dimensional manifold for an intrinsic class of H¨ older functions where the smoothness s can be arbitrarily high.

[Page 2]

There is an existing literature using the Minkowski dimension, also known as the box-counting dimension, of the data domain as a notion of intrinsic dimensionality. The definition of Minkowski dimension is based on the covering number, and manifolds provide an example of a subset having a low Minkowski dimension. In this context, a variety of nonparametric regression and classification algorithms have been studied, including local polynomial regression, k nearest neighbors, Nadaraya-Watson kernel regression, decision trees and least squares kernel ridge regression [ BL07 , Kpo11 , KG13 , KP95 , SN06 , HS21 ]. In comparison, low intrinsic dimensionality beyond the manifold setting in Bayesian nonparametric regression has been less developed (except for [ CE24 ] which uses deep neural networks, see more below). In this work, we derive general conditions to prove posterior contraction rates adaptive to intrinsic data dimensionality measured by the covering number (Minkowski dimension) and thus generalize beyond the manifold assumption.

Posterior contraction rates for Bayesian nonparametric regression have had considerable development since the seminal work of [ GGvdV00 , SW01 ]. Adaptive rates of GP regression for f ∗ defined on [0 , 1] D were proved in [ vdVvZ08b , vdVvZ09 ]. A series of subsequent papers analyzed the performance of Bayesian regression under the assumption of low intrinsic data dimensionality. [ CKP14 ] used a heat kernel on a known manifold within a GP prior and proved the minimax adaptive posterior contraction rate. [ YD16 ] established a minimax optimal adaptive rate in estimating f ∗ on an unknown manifold. The rate adapts to the manifold dimension and smoothness of the regression function, but the function smoothness is restricted to H¨lder class with s ≤ 2 and the prior for the kernel bandwidth parameter requires knowing or estimating the true manifold dimension d . [ DWW22 ] used graph Laplacians to estimate a GP covariance function incorporating the intrinsic geometry of the manifold and proved posterior contraction rates for such GPs, where the regression function lies in a subspace of a Besov space (linearly spanned by eigenfunctions of the manifold Laplace-Beltrami operator).

In addition, [ RBTR24 ] considered GP priors having a Mate´rn kernel on a known compact Riemannian manifold. They proved posterior contraction rates adaptive to the manifold dimension, and when the kernel regularity parameter matches the smoothness of f ∗ the optimal rate can be achieved. Recently, [ RR24 ] considered Bayesian nonparametric regression on an embedded data manifold based on a graph Laplacian eigen-basis, proving an optimal minimax rate adaptive to the manifold dimensionality d and the smoothness s of the regression function f ∗ for arbitrarily high s . Their regression function is in an extrinsic H¨lder class, and the theory requires high regularity of the data density (of H¨lder order s − 1) when the smoothness s of f ∗ is high; the methodology involves eigen-computation of graph Laplacians and their adaptive prior may need the knowledge of d in practice.

Deep GP priors, which improve flexibility over traditional GP priors through several nested layers of GPs, have seen increasing focus in recent years. [ FSH23 ] studied posterior contraction rates for a class of deep GP models, but with a gap between their practical methodology and the theoretical model. [ CR24 ] introduced a deep horseshoe GP prior for data-driven selection of multiscale bandwidths for H¨lder functions on Euclidean domains, while proving posterior contraction rates adaptive to the smoothness of f ∗ and effective dimensionality of the data. For deep GPs with heavy-tailed priors, [ CE24 ] proved posterior contraction rates adaptive to smoothness of f ∗ and the Minkowski dimension of the data domain. Our results show that similar automatic adaptivity to low-dimensional data structures and smoothness of regression functions can also be achieved by more traditional kernel methods, which may have advantages in terms of simplicity.

In this work, we focus on GP priors in which the covariance function, which is in a regularity class that includes the squared exponential, is directly computed from the Euclidean coordinates of X i ’s. We propose an empirical Bayesian prior that adapts to the intrinsic dimensionality of data and the smoothness of f ∗ , without requiring prior knowledge of either. Our main contributions include the following.

• We consider GP regression on a data domain embedded in high dimensional Euclidean space, and establish conditions for nonparametric posterior contraction rates (up to a log factor) adaptive to low intrinsic data dimensionality, measured by the covering number (the Minkowski dimension). We

[Page 3]

propose a GP methodology that satisfies these conditions. The covariance function of the GP is a kernel defined on the ambient Euclidean space belonging to a class of functions that satisfy certain technical conditions, with the squared exponential kernel being a representative example.

• When the data domain is an (unknown) low-dimensional Riemannian manifold, we prove optimal adaptive posterior contraction rates, up to a logarithmic factor, for any order of function smoothness (0 < s < ∞ ). The function regularity order s is measured by a H¨lder class on the manifold which is intrinsically defined. The key element of our analysis is an on-manifold RKHS approximation result of intrinsic manifold H¨lder functions that can go to an arbitrarily high order of s .
• To avoid including knowledge of the intrinsic dimension in the prior, we propose an empirical Bayes approach using kernel affinity and k -nearest neighbor ( k NN) statistics. This approach achieves our theoretical adaptive rates without prior knowledge or estimation of the intrinsic dimensionality of data. The experimental performance of the proposed method is shown on simulated manifold data and image data.


We start by developing a theoretical framework for general low-dimensional data domains having Minkowski dimension ϱ , and then consider the case of manifold data. In the general case, the posterior contraction rate is n − s/ (2 s + ϱ ) , and for d -dimensional submanifolds, the rate becomes n − s/ (2 s + d ) , both up to a log factor. The rate is considered minimax-optimal for the manifold case, and for the general case our result provides an upper bound of the error. In addition, we extend our theory to cover the case of a union of submanifolds having possibly different intrinsic dimensionalities, as an example of simple stratified spaces (of intrinsically low dimension) beyond the setting of a single connected manifold.

The remaining sections of this paper are organized as follows: In Section 2 , we review the necessary background and notation. Section 3 introduces conditions governing the posterior contraction rate of the GP on a general set X . In Section 4 , we prove the optimal contraction rate for H¨lder functions on an unknown manifold and introduce the proposed empirical Bayes prior. Section 5 experimentally evaluates the proposed GP method in comparison to other methods. Finally, Section 6 discusses future extensions. The proofs are deferred to Section 7 and Appendices A B , and the technical lemmas are in Appendix C .

Notations. The notation in this work is standard. a ∨ b = max { a,b } and a ∧ b = min { a,b } . ∥ · ∥ ∞ stands for the ∞ -norm in Euclidean space, or the functional ∞ -norm on C ( X ) where X is the data domain, depending on the context. For the asymptotic notations, f = O ( g ) indicates that there exists a constant C > 0 such that | f | ≤ C | g | in the limit. For non-negative f and g , f ∼ g if there exist C 1 > C 2 > 0 s.t. C 2 g ≤ f ≤ C 1 g in the limit; f ≲ g means that there exists a constant C > 0 such that f ≤ Cg in the limit.

# 2 Preliminaries

In this section, we review background information on Gaussian processes (GP), reproducing kernel Hilbert spaces (RKHS), RKHS on a measurable subset X ⊂ R D , and concepts of Riemannian geometry. Throughout the work, we focus on the case of compact X , and assume that X is a bounded set inside [0 , 1] D without loss of generality.

# 2.1 Reproducing kernel Hilbert Space

Reproducing kernel Hilbert spaces (RKHS) are commonly used in studying the theoretical properties of GPs. See e.g. [ Aro50 , BTA11 ] for a general introduction and [ GvdV17 ] in the context of nonparametric Bayes. In this work, we will rely on some RKHS lemmas in characterizing properties of the posterior. Here, we provide a brief overview of key definitions and concepts.

A symmetric function k : X × X → R is called a positive definite kernel on a non-empty set X if for all n ∈ N , x 1 ,...,x n ∈ X and α 1 ,...,α n ∈ R , the inequality   i   j α i α j k ( x i ,x j ) ≥ 0 holds. Each RKHS on X , denoted by H , corresponds to a unique reproducing kernel k : X × X → R satisfying the property

$$
f ( x ) = \langle f , k ( \cdot , x ) \rangle _ { \mathbb { H } } , \ \forall f \in \mathbb { H } , \ \ x \in \mathbb { X } ,
$$

[Page 4]

where ⟨· , ·⟩ H is the innerproduct of H , and k is positive definite. Conversely, for any given positive definite kernel k , there exists a unique reproducing kernel Hilbert space H in which k serves as the reproducing kernel. The reproducing kernel k can also be represented using the feature map Φ : X → H as k ( x,y ) = ⟨ Φ( x ) , Φ( y ) ⟩ H , and a canonical feature map is Φ( x ) := k ( x, · ). On a set X , a positive definite kernel k uniquely defines an RKHS associated with k .

Functions belonging to an RKHS can be well approximated by linear combinations of functions of the form k ( x i ,x ). More specifically, the set { f : X → R | f ( x ) =   m i =1 a i k ( x i ,x ) , a 1 ,...,a m ∈ R , x 1 ,...,x m ∈ X , m ∈ N } is dense in H . When X is equipped with a measure dx , k and a function g : X → R satisfying integrability conditions, we also have that f ( x ) =   X k ( x,y ) g ( y ) dy is in H and ∥ f ∥ 2 H =   X   X k ( x,y ) g ( x ) g ( y ) dxdy . A formal statement of this property is given in Lemma C.5 where we consider the RKHS on a subset of R D , to be introduced below.

# 2.2 RKHS on a set and subsets

In this work, we consider X = [0 , 1] D , and data samples lie on a subset X ⊂ X . We focus on the squared exponential kernel for ϵ > 0 defined as

$$
h _ { \epsilon } ( x , x ^ { \prime } ) = h \left ( \frac { \| x - x ^ { \prime } \| ^ { 2 } } { \epsilon } \right ) = \exp \left ( - \ \frac { \| x - x ^ { \prime } \| ^ { 2 } } { 2 \epsilon } \right ) ,
$$

where h ( r ) = e − r/ 2 , and ∥·∥ is the Euclidean distance in the ambient space R D . The kernel ( 2 ) can be defined for all pairs of x and x ′ in R D . Our theory applies to a general class of h satisfying technical conditions (Assumption A.3 ), of which the squared exponential kernel is a representative example. D

For any subset S ⊂ [0 , 1] , by restricting to when x,x ′ ∈ S , the kernel h ϵ induces an RKHS on S , which we denote as H ϵ ( S ). This allows us to consider H ϵ ( X ), where X is the data domain. We provide properties of H ϵ ( X ) and the connections between H ϵ ([0 , 1] D ) and H ϵ ( X ) in Appendix C , which will be used in our analysis. This work mainly concerns Gaussian processes and RKHS on the data domain X .

# 2.3 Gaussian process on a general subset X

GPs are widely used as priors for unknown functions. We consider F t x as a centered GP indexed by x ∈ X , where t > 0 is a kernel bandwidth. F t x is determined by the covariance function which is assumed to take the form h t introduced in ( 2 ), that is, E [ F t x F t x ′ ] = h t ( x,x ′ ). Along with a prior p ( t ) on the bandwidth t , the law of the GP provides a prior Π for the unknown regression function f . Using f t to denote the value of f for a specific bandwidth t , we have

$$
f ^ { t } | t \sim G P ( 0 , h _ { t } ( x , x ^ { \prime } ) ) , \ \ t \sim p ( t ) .
$$

The prior p ( t ) will be carefully constructed to obtain adaptive posterior concentration. Our prior Π and the density p ( t ) of the prior on t potentially all depend on n , and we omit the dependence in the notation. n n

Suppose data consist of n observations { X i ,Y i } i =1 . Let f ∈ R denote the values of f at the X i ’s, namely f i = f ( X i ). A GP prior for f implies that the conditional distribution of f given X 1 ,X 2 ,...,X n , denoted as p ( f | X 1 ,X 2 ,...,X n ), follows a Gaussian distribution N (0 , Σ ff ). Here, Σ ff ∈ R n × n represents the covariance matrix induced from the kernel k of the GP, that is, the ( i,j ) element of Σ ff equals Cov( f ( X i ) ,f ( X j )) = k ( X i ,X j ), 1 ≤ i,j ≤ n . By combining the prior distribution N (0 , Σ ff ) with the likelihood function in equation ( 1 ), we can obtain the posterior distribution given the observed data { X i ,Y i } n i =1 , denoted as Π( ·|{ X i ,Y i } n i =1 ). This posterior distribution serves as the foundation for conducting inference and making predictions. Theoretically, we will analyze the posterior contraction rate as well as the convergence of the posterior mean estimator for f ∗ defined as ˆ f ( x ) =   f ( x ) d Π( f |{ X i ,Y i } n i =1 ).

# 2.4 Riemannian manifold and intrinsic derivatives

We introduce some notations of differential geometry that are used in our analysis. All the concepts of Riemannian geometry are standard and can be found in textbooks, e.g., [ DCFF92 , Pet06 ]. Suppose ( M ,g ) is a d -dimensional connected smooth closed (compact and without boundary) Riemannian manifold isometrically embedded in R D through ι : M → R D . When there is no confusion, we also denote ι ( M ) ⊂ R D

[Page 5]

as the manifold M . The metric tensor g is central to the (intrinsic) geometry of M , where we say that a construction is intrinsic if it is fully determined by g (and not by e.g. the embedding mapping ι ). Otherwise, we say an object is extrinsic . For example, the geodesic distance d M ( x,y ), the Riemannian volume dV , the injectivity radius ξ , normal coordinates, the Riemannian connection ∇ and covariant derivatives are all intrinsic; In contrast, the second fundamental form II and the manifold reach τ are both associated with ι ( M ) and are extrinsic. A detailed review of the notations of d M ( x,y ), dV , ξ , the exponential map exp x , normal coordinates, geodesic curve, and covariant derivatives can be found in Appendix C.2 . Below we elaborate more on the intrinsic derivatives of a differentiable function on M . k

For f ∈ C ( M ), there are different ways to consider the derivatives of f on M . A common way is to parametrize f on a geodesic-ball neighborhood of x in normal coordinates, that is, to consider the composed function ˜ f := f ◦ exp x as a C k function on B R d ξ (0) and then use the standard derivatives of ˜ f in R d . In this work, we heavily use the covariant derivative induced by the Riemannian (Levi-Civita) connection ∇ . The k -th covariant derivative of f , denoted as ∇ k f , is an orderk tensor field on M . See Appendix C.2 for the formal definition and the concepts of vector/tensor fields on M . d ˜

The covariant derivative is closely related to the R -derivative of f ( u ) in that the two “coincide” at u = 0. Specifically, let u be the normal coordinates of M at x , u ∈ T x M ∼ = R d , and ˜ f ( u ) = f (exp x ( u )) is C k on B R d ξ (0). For v 1 , ··· ,v k ∈ T x M , we equivalently denote by v i the vector in R d . Then the covariant derivative ∇ k f ( x ) as an orderk tensor on T x M×···× T x M can be written as ∇ k f ( x )( v 1 , ··· ,v k ) = D k ˜ f (0)( v 1 , ··· ,v k ) , where D is the standard derivative in R d and D k ˜ f (0) is an orderk tensor in R d . A useful consequence is that when f ∈ C k ( M ), D k ˜ f (0) is a real symmetric tensor due to symmetry of the partials. Then the representation of ∇ k f ( x ) by D k ˜ f (0) allows us to use the spectral norm of the symmetric tensor to define the operator norm of ∇ k f ( x ). Specifically, by Banach’s Theorem [ Ban38 ], we have

$$
\sup _ { v _ { 1 } , \cdots , v _ { k } \in S ^ { d - 1 } \subset T _ { x } \mathcal { M } } | \nabla ^ { k } f ( x ) ( v _ { 1 } , \cdots , v _ { k } ) | = \sup _ { v \in S ^ { d - 1 } \subset T _ { x } \mathcal { M } } | \nabla ^ { k } f ( x ) ( v , \cdots , v ) | ,
$$

which is defined to be ∥∇ k f ( x ) ∥ op . While the above equality is derived using the normal-coordinate representation of ∇ k f ( x ), this definition of ∥∇ k f ( x ) ∥ op is intrinsic and independent of the choice of local coordinates at x nor basis of T x M .

The covariant derivative is also closely related to the parallel transport (induced by ∇ ). In this work, we will use the parallel transport along the unique minimizing geodesic curve. Specifically, for y ∈ B ξ ( x ), the parallel transport along the unique minimizing geodesic from x to y is a mapping P x,y : T x M → T y M . Recall that the (unique) minimizing geodesic from x to y can be written as γ ( t ) = exp x ( tv ), where γ (0) = x , γ ( d M ( x,y )) = y , and ˙ γ (0) = v is a unit vector in T x M , We say that a vector field U is parallel along γ if ∇ ˙ γ U = 0 along γ , and this is equivalent to that U ( γ ( t )) = P x,γ ( t ) U (0). In other words, for any w ∈ T x M , define W by W ( γ ( t )) := P x,γ ( t ) w , then W is parallel along γ (the vector field W is only defined on γ but this suffices here). In particular, ˙ γ ( t ) is parallel along γ , namely, ∇ ˙ γ ˙ γ = 0 along γ . k

k

We will use the above concepts of ∥∇ f ( x ) ∥ op and the parallel transport P x,y , both of which are intrinsic, to define our H¨lder class on M in Section 4 . A similar construction of a manifold H¨lder class was introduced in [ KP21 ]. Before the end of this subsection, we further comment on the Euclidean derivatives D k ˜ f and compare with the covariant derivatives ∇ k f . Because the exponential map and normal coordinates are intrinsic, the derivatives D k ˜ f on B R d ξ (0) are also intrinsically defined and are hence a type of intrinsic derivatives. However, when u ̸ = 0, D k ˜ f ( u ) generally does not equal ∇ k f (exp x ( u )). This means that quantities like sup u ∈ B R d ξ (0) ∥ D k ˜ f ( u ) ∥ op , which is defined on a geodesic-ball neighborhood of x , differ from (is larger than) our notion of ∥∇ k f ( x ) ∥ op . A manifold H¨lder class was previously defined using the (Euclidean) H¨lder norm of ˜ f , see e.g. [ RR24 ]. Such a definition utilizes an atlas cover of the manifold and considers the (Euclidean) H¨lder norm of ˜ f on each atlas. The resulting H¨lder norm thus depends on the chosen atlas cover (though the norms from different atlas covers are equivalent so the the resulting H¨lder class is the same). In contrast, our definition of H¨lder norm does not involve any choice of atlas. While our H¨lder norm differs from those defined from ˜ f , the H¨lder norms should be equivalent, i.e. bounded by a constant from each other. In this work, our notion of H¨lder norm facilitates the parallel transport techniques used in the quantitative analysis of the H¨lder norms.

# 3 Posterior contraction rates: general result

In this section, we prove the general result of posterior contraction rates adaptive to the intrinsic low dimensionality of the observed data. We will show that the posterior contraction rate is at least n − s/ (2 s + ϱ ) up to a logarithmic factor, where s depends on the approximation property of the true function f ∗ , and ϱ is the intrinsic low dimensionality of data. The formal characterization will be detailed in Assumption 1 . These are the most general conditions to show the adaptive posterior contraction rate in this paper, and we will focus on the manifold case in Section 4 .

# 3.1 Definitions and general assumptions

We introduce our assumptions on the data distribution and true function f ∗ in the regression model ( 1 ). We first define the posterior contraction rate. Let { X i ,Y i } n i =1 denote the observed data, and recall that Π( A |{ X i ,Y i } n i =1 ) denotes the posterior of an event A under the prior Π as in ( 3 ). Let d n be a semi-metric measuring the discrepancy between f and f ∗ . Following [ GGvdV00 , GvdV07 , vdVvZ09 ], we say that the posterior contraction rate of the GP prior with respect to d n is at least ε n if

$$
\Pi ( d _ { n } ( f , f ^ { * } ) > \varepsilon _ { n } | \{ X _ { i } , Y _ { i } \} _ { i = 1 } ^ { n } ) \to 0 \ \ i n \ p r o b a b i l { i t y } \ a s \ n \to \infty .
$$

We recall the notation of covering numbers. Generally, suppose ( E, ∥ · ∥ ) is a normed space and S ⊂ E . Given ε > 0, N ⊂ E is called an ε -net of S if ∀ x ∈ S , ∃ s ∈ N , s.t. ∥ x − s ∥ ≤ ε . The covering number N ( ε,S, ∥ · ∥ ) of S (under norm ∥ · ∥ ) is defined to be the minimum cardinality of an ε -net of S . Namely, N ( ε,S, ∥·∥ ) = min { m ∈ N : ∃ s 1 , ··· ,s m ∈ E, s . t .S ⊂ ∪ m i =1 ¯ B ∥·∥ ( s i ,ε ) } , where ¯ B ∥·∥ ( s,ε ) := { x ∈ E, ∥ x − s ∥ ≤ ε } is the ε -ball centered at s under norm ∥·∥ . We are ready to introduce the assumptions on the data domain X and f ∗ .

Assumption 1. For positive constants ϱ and s ,

- (A1) Intrinsic low-dimensionality of X : X ⊂ [0 , 1] D and there exist positive constants C X and r 0 ∈ (0 , 1) that may depend on X , s.t. ϱ

$$
\mathcal { N } ( r , \mathcal { X } , \| \cdot \| _ { \infty } ) \leq C _ { \mathcal { X } } r ^ { - \varrho } , \ \forall r \in ( 0 , r _ { 0 } ] .
$$

- (A2) Approximation of f ∗ by RKHS: There exist positive constants ϵ 0 , ν 1 , ν 2 that may depend on X and f ∗ , s.t. for all ϵ < ϵ 0 , there is a function F ϵ ∈ H ϵ ( X ) such that


$$
\sup _ { x \in \mathcal { X } } | F ^ { \epsilon } ( x ) - f ^ { * } ( x ) | \leq \nu _ { 1 } \epsilon ^ { s / 2 } , \ \| F ^ { \epsilon } \| _ { \mathbb { H } _ { \epsilon } ( \mathcal { X } ) } ^ { 2 } \leq \nu _ { 2 } \epsilon ^ { - \ell / 2 } .
$$

In (A1), ∥·∥ ∞ denotes the ∞ -norm in R D . One can define the covering number of X using another norm d D in R D , however, as long as the d D -unit ball is contained in the ∥·∥ ∞ -unit ball, we have N ( r, X , ∥·∥ ∞ ) ≤ N ( r, X ,d D ). This includes the case when d D is the p -norm for any p ≥ 1 in R D , and our assumption ( 4 ) is weaker. The factor ϱ in (A1) corresponds to the intrinsic dimensionality of data, though technically it is an upper bound on dimensionality in the small r limit. The limit of log N ( r, X , ∥ · ∥ ∞ ) / log(1 /r ) as r → 0+ is the Minkowski or box-counting dimension [ Fal04 , HS21 ]. If the sup-limit exists and equals ϱ 0 , ϱ 0 = inf { ρ ≥ 0 , limsup r → 0+ N ( r, X , ∥ · ∥ ∞ ) r ρ = 0 } is called the upper Minkowski dimension of X , then (A1) holds with ϱ = ϱ 0 + ε for any ε > 0. The condition (A1) also holds with ϱ = ϱ 0 if limsup r → 0+ N ( r, X , ∥ · ∥ ∞ ) r ϱ 0 = 0. Our proved rate involves a dimension factor ϱ that either equals to or can be arbitrarily close to the (upper) Minkowski dimension of X . The factor s in (A2) corresponds to the smoothness of f ∗ . We will show in Section 4 that (A2) holds when X is a smooth manifold and f ∗ is a C s H¨lder function on X . Generally, the condition (A2) requires certain regularity of f ∗ on X . D

Intuitively, the assumption (A1) asks the subset X to occupy a small portion of the ambient space R such that the intrinsic complexity of X is lower than D . This holds when X is restricted to a subspace, which is equivalent to some notion of sparsity. Subspaces are linear subsets of low dimensionality, and assumption (A1) also covers non-linear cases, such as the important case when X is a sub-manifold or stratified space; see the examples below.

Example 3.1 (Low-dimensional manifold) . Let X = M ⊂ [0 , 1] D be a d -dimensional compact smooth Riemannian manifold isometrically embedded in R D with d ∈ N , d ≤ D . One can construct an r -covering of M which satisfies N ( r, M , ∥ · ∥ ∞ ) ≤ C M r -d for all small enough r , see e.g. [HS21]. (A1) also holds for a submanifold which has less regularity or with boundary, e.g. the unit cube [0 , 1] d satisfies (A1) with ϱ = d . Similarly, it holds when (the bounded set) X is restricted to a low-dimensional subspace in R D .

[Page 7]

Because our notion of low dimensionality in (A1) is Minkowski dimension-like and characterized by the covering number, it is more general than the manifold assumption used in the previous manifold regression literature, such as [ BL07 , YD16 ].

Example 3.2 (Stratified space) . A stratified space [ Wei94 ] generalizes the concept of a manifold by allowing for more complicated geometric structures, in particular, consisting of different “strata” or “layers,” each having its own well-behaved geometric properties. The covering dimension of a stratified space measures its topological complexity. It is the maximum dimension among the strata. A stratified space has a finite covering dimension if and only if all of its strata have finite dimensions. A simple example is X = M 1   M 2 where M 1 , M 2 ⊂ [0 , 1] D are two compact connected smooth Riemannian manifolds having dimensions d 1 ,d 2 ∈ Z + , respectively. One can verify that in this case X satisfies assumption (A1) with ϱ = max { d 1 ,d 2 } , see Lemma B.7 .

# 3.2 Dimension-adaptive prior of kernel bandwidth

The prior on the kernel bandwidth ϵ > 0 in ( 2 ) has a key impact on the posterior contraction rate. We denote the prior as p ( t ), for t = ϵ > 0. For the posterior contraction rate to be adaptive to the intrinsic dimensionality ϱ , the prior p ( t ) needs to be carefully constructed.

We introduce a dimension-adaptive prior condition in Assumption 2 , which allows us to bypass the need to condition on ϱ in the prior for the bandwidth; such conditioning is common practice in the literature with the rescaled Gamma prior providing a notable case, see Example 3.3 . In Section 4.3 we propose an empirical Bayes prior that satisfies Assumption 2 when data lie on a submanifold. The prior p ( t ) can depend on sample size n and data { X i } n i =1 , and we omit such dependence for the brevity of notation.

Assumption 2 (Condition on the prior of bandwidth ϵ ) . (A3) Given positive constants ϱ and s , there exist c 2 > c 1 > 0 and c 3 ,a 1 ,a 2 ,K 1 ,K 2 ,C 1 ,C 2 > 0 , such that

$$
p ( t ) \geq C _ { 1 } t ^ { - a _ { 1 } } \exp \left ( - \, \frac { K _ { 1 } } { t ^ { e / 2 } } \right ) , \ \forall \ t \ s . t . \ c _ { 1 } \leq t / \left ( n ^ { - 2 / ( 2 s + e ) } ( \log n ) ^ { \frac { 2 ( 1 + D ) } { 2 s + e } } \right ) \leq c _ { 2 } ,
$$

$$
p ( t ) \leq C _ { 2 } t ^ { - a _ { 2 } } \exp \left ( - \, \frac { K _ { 2 } } { t ^ { e / 2 } } \right ) , \ \forall t \ s . t . \ 0 < t / \left ( n ^ { - 2 / ( 2 s + e ) } ( \log n ) ^ { \frac { - 4 ( 1 + D ) } { ( 2 + e / s ) \ell } } \right ) \leq c _ { 3 } .
$$

The inequality ( 6 ) ensures that p ( t ) is sufficiently large for t ∼ n − 2 / (2 s + ϱ ) , and ( 7 ) ensures that p ( t ) is close to zero when t is smaller than the order of n − 2 / (2 s + ϱ ) . To provide a prior p ( t ) that satisfies (A3), one method is to use a rescaled Gamma prior when the intrinsic dimensionality ϱ is known, as shown in the next example.

Example 3.3 (Rescaled Gamma prior [ vdVvZ09 ]) . Let Ga( a 0 ,b 0 ) denote the gamma distribution with probability density function proportional to t a 0 − 1 e − b 0 t , where a 0 ,b 0 > 0 are two constants. The rescaled Gamma prior of ϵ = t is such that t − ϱ/ 2 follows the distribution of Ga( a 0 ,b 0 ) . As has been shown in [ vdVvZ09 ], this prior p ( t ) satisfies both of the inequalities in (A3) for all t > 0 , and as a result, this prior satisfies (A3) for any s ∈ (0 , ∞ ) .

For Bayesian manifold regression, the rescaled Gamma prior was adopted in [ YD16 ]; they estimate the manifold dimension when not known. While methods are available for manifold dimension estimation [ LB04 , FSA07 ], they may encounter difficulties in practice, especially when the sample size is small. We discuss the manifold data case in more detail in Section 4 . In Section 4.3 , we will propose an empirical Bayes prior computed using kernel affinities and k -nearest neighbor techniques and theoretically show that our prior can satisfy (A3) with high probability (Proposition 4.4 ). As a result, our prior can achieve the adaptive contraction rate without knowledge of the intrinsic manifold dimension. In practice, our prior can perform more stably than the rescaled Gamma prior with estimated manifold dimension.

[Page 8]

# 3.3 The general result of adaptive rates

We are ready to prove the dimension-adaptive posterior contraction rate. The proofs are provided in Appendix A . n

We consider two scenarios, the fixed design where the predictors { X i } i =1 are given and fixed, and the random design where the marginal distribution of X i is P X on X . Under the fixed design, the residual w i | X ∼ N (0 ,σ 2 ) are independent across i , and as a result the variables Y i | X are also independent (but not i.i.d.). To measure the discrepancy between f and the ground truth f ∗ , the in-sample mean squared error is defined as ∥ f − f ∗ ∥ 2 n := 1 n   n i =1 ( f ( X i ) − f ∗ ( X i )) 2 , which is well-defined under the fixed design. Under the random design, we also define the population error as ∥ f − f ∗ ∥ 2 2 :=   X ( f ( x ) − f ∗ ( x )) 2 P X ( dx ).

Theorem 3.1 (Fixed design posterior contraction rate) . Suppose Assumptions 1 2 are satisfied with the same positive factors ϱ and s . Then, there exists a positive constant C s.t. the posterior contraction rate with respect to ∥ · ∥ n is at least ¯ ε n = Cn − s 2 s + ϱ (log n ) D +1 2+ ϱ/s + D +1 2 ≲ n − s/ (2 s + ϱ ) (log n ) D +1 .

Recall that the posterior mean of f is defined as ˆ f ( x ) =   f t ( x ) d Π( f t |{ X i ,Y i } n i =1 ). The next theorem shows that the estimator ˆ f achieves the same adaptive rate of convergence under the fixed design.

Theorem 3.2 (Fixed design estimator convergence rate) . Under the assumption of Theorem 3.1 with ¯ ε n as therein, suppose f ∗ is bounded on X . Then, with probability tending to one, ∥ ˆ f − f ∗ ∥ n ≤ 3¯ ε n .

To extend the theory to random design, we will adopt a truncation of f as originally proposed in [ YD16 ]. We will assume a known upper bound of f ∗ , that is, ∥ f ∗ ∥ ∞ ≤ M , and this M can be any upper bound of ∥ f ∗ ∥ ∞ . For a function f and A > 0, define f A := ( f ∨ ( − A )) ∧ A . We will consider the posterior of f M , and the corresponding posterior mean estimator is defined as ˆ f M ( x ) :=   f M ( x ) d Π( f |{ X i ,Y i } n i =1 ). The theoretical necessity of the truncation lies in that we will use empirical process techniques to bound ∥ f − f ∗ ∥ 2 by comparing to ∥ f − f ∗ ∥ n , which would require the function class to be bounded to begin with.

With the truncation, the next theorem proves the adaptive posterior contraction rate and the posteriormean estimator convergence rate under the random design.

Theorem 3.3 (Random design) . Suppose Assumptions 1 2 are satisfied with the same positive factors ϱ and s , and X i are i.i.d. samples drawn from some distribution P X on X . In addition, for some positive constant M , ∥ f ∗ ∥ ∞ ≤ M . Let ¯ ε n be as in Theorem 3.1 . Then, there exists an absolute constant c s.t.

$$
\Pi ( \| f _ { M } - f ^ { * } \| _ { 2 } > c ( M + 1 ) \bar { \varepsilon } _ { n } | \{ X _ { i } , Y _ { i } \} _ { i = 1 } ^ { n } ) \to 0 \ \ i n \ p r o b a b i l i t y \ a s \ n \to \infty .
$$

Moreover, with probability tending to one, || ˆ f M − f ∗ || 2 ≤ c ( M + 1)¯ ε n .

In Theorems 3.1 3.3 , the rate n − s/ (2 s + ϱ ) only depends on s and ϱ , and the ambient dimensionality D appears in the logarithmic terms. The constant C depends on ϱ , s , X , D , the kernel function h , and also the constants in (A1)(A2)(A3), and the dependence can be tracked in the proofs. In Appendix A.3 , we also extend our theory through a relaxation of Assumption 2 (A3) that replaces ϱ with an upper bound ϱ + in ( 6 ) and a lower bound ϱ − in ( 7 ) respectively, see Assumption A.1 . This can be intuitively understood as a “misspecification” of ϱ between ϱ ± . When ϱ ± does not equal to ϱ , the contraction rate will degenerate into ε n ∼ n − r ( ϱ,ϱ ± ,s ) (up to a logarithmic factor) where r ( ϱ,ϱ ± ,s ) is worse than s/ (2 s + ϱ ), and we recover the s/ (2 s + ϱ ) rate when ϱ ± = ϱ , see Theorem A.3 . This theoretical extension allows to establish adaptive posterior contraction rates when using a prior p ( t ) not exactly but close to satisfying (A3), e.g., the rescaled Gamma prior with a misspecified ϱ .

# 4 Adaptive rates for data on manifolds

In this section, we focus on the special case where data samples lie on a smooth closed Riemannian manifold of intrinsic dimensionality d . We will obtain in Section 4.2 the optimal posterior contraction rate O ( n − s/ (2 s + ϱ ) ), up to a logarithmic factor, when f ∗ is a C k,β -H¨lder function on the manifold, ϱ = d and the smoothness order s = k + β > 0 can be any positive number. This relies on a key manifold RKHS approximation result proved in Section 4.1 .

[Page 9]

In Section 4.3 , we propose a new empirical Bayes prior based on kernel affinity and k NN statistics, which enables us to achieve the optimal rate without knowledge of the manifold intrinsic dimension d . The proofs are postponed to Section 7 and Appendix B .

# 4.1 RKHS approximation of Ho¨lder functions on manifold

We provide a manifold RKHS approximation result to ensure Assumption 1 (A2). We show RKHS approximation of a class of manifold H¨lder functions that are intrinsically defined; this result can be of independent interest.

Assumption 3 (Data manifold) . The data domain X = M is a d -dimensional smooth connected closed Riemannian manifold isometrically embedded in [0 , 1] D ⊂ R D .

As will be shown in the proof of Lemma 4.1 , it suffices to have C max { 2 k, 3 } regularity of M instead of C ∞ when approximating a target function f ∈ C k,β ( M ). We say that a manifold M is C r , r being a positive integer, when both the Riemannian metric g and the embedding map ι are at least C r . In Assumption 3 , the connectedness of M can be removed, which is equivalent to when X is a disjoint union of connected manifolds. Our theory can extend to such cases where each manifold can have distinct dimensionalities, see Appendix B.3 . In this section, we assume smoothness and connectedness of M for simplicity.

Recall the differential geometry notations in Section 2.4 , and in particular, the definition of covariant derivatives (with respect to the Riemannian connection ∇ ) and the parallel transport P x,y along unique minimizing geodesics within the geodesic ball B ξ ( x ), ξ > 0 being the injectivity radius of M . To simplify notation, when the k -th covariant derivative at x is applied to the same vector v ∈ T x M for k many times, we introduce the notation ∇ k v f ( x ) := ∇ k f ( x )( v, ··· ,v ) . Given a non-negative integer k , suppose f ∈ C k ( M ), we define ∥∇ k f ( x ) ∥ op := sup v ∈ S d − 1 ⊂ T x M |∇ k v f ( x ) | , and ∥∇ k f ∥ ∞ := sup x ∈M ∥∇ k f ( x ) ∥ op . For 0 < β ≤ 1, we define k k β

$$
L _ { k , \beta } ( f , x ) \coloneqq \sup _ { y \in B _ { \xi } ( x ) } \sup _ { v \in S ^ { d - 1 } \subset T _ { x } \mathcal { M } } | \nabla _ { v } ^ { k } f ( x ) - \nabla _ { P _ { x , y } v } ^ { k } f ( y ) | / d _ { \mathcal { M } } ^ { \beta } ( x , y ) ,
$$

and we further define L k,β ( f ) := sup x ∈M L k,β ( f,x ).

Definition 1 (Ho¨lder class on manifold) . For k = 0 , 1 , ··· , 0 < β ≤ 1 , and f ∈ C k ( M ) , the H¨older norm of f is defined as ∥ f ∥ k,β :=   k l =0 ∥∇ l f ∥ ∞ + L k,β ( f ) . We say f ∈ C k,β ( M ) whenever ∥ f ∥ k,β < ∞ .

Recall the kernel function defined in ( 2 ), where h ( r ) = e − r/ 2 ; M is embedded in R D through ι : M → [0 , 1] D . For ϵ > 0 and f ∈ L 1 ( M ), we define the on-manifold integral operator G ϵ : L 1 ( M ) → L 1 ( M ) as

$$
G _ { \epsilon } ( f ) ( x ) \colon = \frac { 1 } { ( 2 \pi \epsilon ) ^ { d / 2 } } \int _ { \mathcal { M } } h \left ( \frac { \| \iota ( x ) - \iota ( y ) \| _ { \mathbb { R } ^ { D } } ^ { 2 } } { \epsilon } \right ) f ( y ) d V ( y ) .
$$

Lemma 4.1. Under Assumption 3 , given non-negative integer k and 0 < β ≤ 1 , there exists a constant ϵ 1 ( M ,d,k ) such that when ϵ < ϵ 1 , for any f ∈ C k,β ( M ) , there exist f j ∈ C k − 2 j,β ( M ) , j = 1 , ··· , ⌊ k/ 2 ⌋ , and R f,ϵ ∈ C ( M ) s.t.

$$
G _ { \epsilon } ( f ) ( x ) = f ( x ) + \sum _ { j = 1 } ^ { \lfloor k / 2 \rfloor } \epsilon ^ { j } f _ { j } ( x ) + R _ { f , \epsilon } ( x ) ,
$$

- (i) The remainder term R f,ϵ satisfies ∥ R f,ϵ ∥ ∞ ≤ ˜ C 1 ( M ,d,k ) ∥ f ∥ k,β ϵ ( k + β ) / 2 ;
- (ii) For all 0 ≤ j ≤ ⌊ k/ 2 ⌋ , ∥ f j ∥ k − 2 j,β ≤ ˜ C 2 ( M ,d,k ) ∥ f ∥ k,β (when j = 0 , we set f 0 = f );


where the constants ˜ C 1 ( M ,d,k ) and ˜ C 2 ( M ,d,k ) are determined by d , k , and the manifold geometry. Specifically, both constants depend on the bounds of the curvature tensor of M , the second fundamental form of ι ( M ) and their covariant derivatives; ˜ C 1 ( M ,d,k ) also depends on the volume of M and bounds of intrinsic derivatives of the Riemann metric tensor; ˜ C 2 ( M ,d,k ) also depends on the diameter of M . The small ϵ threshold ϵ 1 depends on d , k , the injectivity radius of M and the reach of ι ( M ) .

[Page 10]

Our analysis characterizes the specific dependence of the constants on the manifold geometry, including both intrinsic and extrinsic quantities. Lemma 4.1 can be proved for a broader class of h that satisfies differentiability and subexponential decay on [0 , ∞ ), namely Assumption A.3 (i), see Remark 2 . This will suffice for the RKHS approximation results in Proposition 4.2 which are based on Lemma 4.1 . For the RKHS covering estimates to hold, one will need additional conditions (sub-exponential decay and radial monotonicity) on the spectral density of the kernel. The technical conditions on the kernel function are summarized in Assumption A.3 . The square exponential kernel corresponding to h ( r ) = e − r/ 2 is a typical example satisfying these assumptions and also widely used in practice.

The next proposition constructs an RKHS approximation of a H¨lder function f on M .

Proposition 4.2. Under Assumption 3 , given non-negative integer k and 0 < β ≤ 1 , there exists a constant ϵ 2 ( M ,d,k ) such that when ϵ < ϵ 2 , for any f ∈ C k,β ( M ) , we can find F =   ⌊ k/ 2 ⌋ i =0 ϵ i F i with F i ∈ C k − 2 i,β ( M ) , and ( k + β ) / 2

$$
\| G _ { \epsilon } ( F ) - f \| _ { \infty } \leq \gamma _ { 1 } ( \mathcal { M } , d , k ) \| f \| _ { k , \beta } \epsilon ^ { ( k + \beta ) / 2 } ,
$$

$$
\| G _ { \epsilon } ( F ) \| _ { \mathbb { H } _ { \epsilon } ( \mathcal { M } ) } ^ { 2 } \leq \gamma _ { 2 } ( \mathcal { M } , d , k ) \| f \| _ { k , \beta } ^ { 2 } \epsilon ^ { - d / 2 } ,
$$

where both constants γ 1 ( M ,d,k ) and γ 2 ( M ,d,k ) depend on d , k , and the manifold geometry, inheriting the M -dependence from the constants ˜ C 1 ( M ,d,k ) and ˜ C 2 ( M ,d,k ) in Lemma 4.1 . The threshold ϵ 2 depends on d , k and inherits the M -dependence from the thresholds ϵ 1 ( M ,d,k ) in Lemma 4.1 .

The proof of Proposition 4.2 is based on the expansion ( 9 ) provided by Lemma 4.1 , and it uses a high order correction scheme. Proposition 4.2 provides the RKHS approximation and Hilbert norm control needed in Assumption 1 (A2) when data domain X is a manifold and f ∗ belongs to the manifold H¨lder class. Specifically, the equations ( 10 ) and ( 11 ) correspond to the condition ( 5 ) in (A2), where s = k + β > 0 and ϱ = d . We will use Proposition 4.2 in Section 4.2 in obtaining the adaptive posterior contraction rate.

Our RKHS approximation result is stronger than needed by the posterior contraction analysis. This firstly lies in the requirement on the smallness of ϵ . In (A2), the needed small bandwidth threshold ϵ 0 is allowed to depend on X = M and f ∗ , while in Proposition 4.2 the threshold ϵ 2 ( M ) only depends on M . In other words, we have shown that once ϵ is less than a threshold that only depends on M , the approximation bounds in Proposition 4.2 hold uniformly for all f in the H¨lder class instead of a specific target function to approximate. In addition, while it suffices to show the existence of constants ν 1 , ν 2 in (A2), in Proposition 4.2 , we separate the constants dependence on f and M in the two bounds ( 10 ) and ( 11 ). In each bound, the constant has a factor proportional to the H¨lder norm of f multiplied by a factor that only depends on manifold geometric quantities.

# 4.2 Posterior contraction for Ho¨lder functions

Combining Proposition 4.2 with Theorems 3.1 and 3.3 allows us to prove the optimal contraction rate for any f ∗ ∈ C k,β ( M ).

Assumption 4 (Ho¨lder regression function) . The true function f ∗ ∈ C k,β ( M ) for some k = 0 , 1 , ··· and 0 < β ≤ 1 .

Corollary 4.3. Under Assumptions 3 4 , and suppose that the prior on the kernel bandwidth ϵ satisfies Assumption 2 with ϱ = d and s = k + β .

- (i) Fixed design. There exists a positive constant C s.t. the posterior contraction rate for ∥ f − f ∗ ∥ n is at least ¯ ε n = Cn − s 2 s + d (log n ) D +1 2+ d/s + D +1 2 ≲ n − s/ (2 s + d ) (log n ) D +1 . Furthermore, if f ∗ is bounded on X , then with probability tending to one, ∥ ˆ f − f ∗ ∥ n ≤ 3¯ ε n .
- (ii) Random design. Suppose X i are i.i.d. samples drawn from some distribution P X on M , and for some positive constant M , ∥ f ∗ ∥ ∞ ≤ M . Then, there exists an absolute constant c s.t. the posterior contraction rate for ∥ f M − f ∗ ∥ 2 is at least c ( M + 1)¯ ε n , and with probability tending to one, || ˆ f M − f ∗ || 2 ≤ c ( M + 1)¯ ε n .


Remark 1 (Extension to stratified space) . We focus on posterior contraction rates for H¨ older functions on a single manifold, while our analysis can be extended to stratified spaces (Example 3.2). In Appendix B.3, we extend the theory to when X is a finite union of disjoint manifolds (of possibly different dimensions) and there is a constant separation between the strata. The extension is by firstly extending Lemma 4.1 with a modified definition of G ϵ ( f ), and then Proposition 4.2 and Corollary 4.3 follow with essentially the same proofs. In the convergence rates, the intrinsic dimension ϱ equals the maximum dimension of the manifolds. Extension to more complex stratified spaces is left for future work.

[Page 11]

# 4.3 Empirical Bayes prior on kernel bandwidth

In this subsection, we propose a bandwidth prior p ( t ) that satisfies Assumption 2 (A3) and does not require knowledge of the intrinsic dimension d of the manifold. In practice, our prior can give more stable performance than the previous approach based on estimating the manifold dimension [ YD16 ], see the experimental comparison in Appendix D.3 .

Our empirical Bayes prior on the bandwidth t takes the form

$$
p ( t ) \, \in t ^ { - a _ { 0 } } \exp \left ( - \, \frac { b _ { 0 } } { \hat { v } _ { n } ( t ) } \right ) \, w h e n \, \ \gamma _ { 1 } T _ { n } ^ { 2 } < t \leq 1 ; \ \ p ( t ) = 0 \ o t h e r w i s e ,
$$

where a 0 ,b 0 > 0 are arbitrary hyperparameters, γ 1 is a positive constant (in our experiments set to be 1 / 4), T n is computed from averaged k -nearest neighbor ( k NN) distances, and ˆ v n ( t ) is the averaged kernel affinity to be specified below. 2

Specifically, for some subset S of [ n ] := { 1 , ··· ,n } and k ≤ n , we let k = ⌈ γ 2 log ( n ) ⌉ where γ 2 is a positive constant (set to be 1/4 in our experiments), and define

$$
T _ { n } \coloneqq \frac { 1 } { | S | } \sum _ { i \in S } \hat { R } _ { k } ( X _ { i } ) , \quad \hat { R } _ { k } ( x ) \coloneqq \inf _ { r } \left \{ r > 0 , \ s . t . \ \sum _ { j = 1 } ^ { n } 1 _ { \{ \| X _ { j } - x \| < r \} } \geq k \right \} ,
$$

namely ˆ R k ( X i ) is the distance from X i to its k -th nearest neighbor in the n samples { X j } n j =1 ( X i is its own 1st nearest neighbor). Our theory permits S to be an arbitrary subset, and we explain the choice in practice at the end of this subsection. In our experiments, when n is small (less than 200) we let k = 2.

The quantity ˆ v n ( t ) is an averaged kernel affinity defined as

$$
\hat { v } _ { n } ( t ) \coloneqq \frac { 1 } { n ( n - 1 ) } \sum _ { i = 1 } ^ { n } \sum _ { j \neq i } h _ { t } ( X _ { i } , X _ { j } ) ,
$$

̸

where h t ( x,x ′ ) is defined in ( 2 ). ˆ v n ( t ) is a function of the bandwidth t > 0. We omit the normalizing constant ˆ Z n :=   1 γ 1 T 2 n t − a 0 exp   − b 0 ˆ v n ( t )   dt from ( 12 ), since it is not needed in computational implementations based on Markov Chain Monte Carlo (MCMC) sampling. ˆ Z n will be analyzed in our theoretical analysis.

The proposed prior p ( t ) does not require knowledge of either the intrinsic dimension ϱ = d or the regularity order s . The empirical statistics T n and ˆ v n ( t ) used in constructing p ( t ) contain information on d implicitly. Our analysis will show that under our setting, ˆ v n ( t ) ∼ t d/ 2 when t > Cn − 2 /d (up to a logarithmic factor), and T 2 n ∼ n − 2 /d (up to a logarithmic factor). The validity of the proposed prior p ( t ) will be proved in Proposition 4.4 , and we need certain assumptions on the data density. We denote by p X the probability density function with respect to the base measure dV on M , where dV is the intrinsic Riemannian volume.

Assumption 5 (Boundedness of data density) . p X is uniformly bounded from below and above, that is, for positive constants p min ,p max , 0 < p min ≤ p X ( x ) ≤ p max , ∀ x ∈ M .

Proposition 4.4 (Validity of the empirical prior) . Under Assumption 3 , suppose X i are i.i.d. samples drawn from density p X on M where p X ∈ C 2 ( M ) and satisfies Assumption 5 . Let ϱ = d , given any s > 0 and a 0 , b 0 , γ 1 , γ 2 some fixed positive constants, there exists n 0 ( M ,p X ,s ) such that when n > n 0 , let p ( t ) be as in ( 12 )( 13 )( 14 ) with S being an arbitrary subset of [ n ] in the definition of T n and k = ⌈ γ 2 log 2 ( n ) ⌉ , then, with probability ≥ 1 − 2 n − 10 , the prior p ( t ) satisfies Assumption 2 with ϱ and s , where the constants c 1 , K 1 , etc. can be properly specified.

The proposition shows that the proposed p ( t ) satisfies the needed condition in Corollary 4.3 . As a result, this prior leads to the optimal posterior contraction rate that is automatically adaptive to s and d . When X

is a disjoint union of manifolds, we can extend Proposition 4.4 after a modification of the definition of ˆ v n ( t ), see Appendix B.3 .

The prior p ( t ) can be computed without incurring more expensive computation than other steps in the Bayesian regression. Specifically, the kernel affinity statistic ˆ v n ( t ) sums the (off-diagonal) entries of the kernel matrix, which is of lower computational complexity than constructing the kernel and computing the posterior mean of f . The k NN statistic ˆ R k ( X i ) can be computed by standard subroutines and the computational complexity is less than other kernel operations, and we compute the k NN distance for | S | points as in ( 13 ). While our theory allows | S | to be arbitrarily small, in practice, a larger | S | can potentially help reduce variance and improve the algorithm’s stability at finite sample sizes. The primary limitation of using a larger subset S is increased computational cost. More algorithmic details of the Bayesian regression are provided in Appendix D.1 .

# 5 Numerical experiments

We numerically implement Bayesian inference on various datasets and compare the proposed method, namely the empirical Bayes prior on the kernel bandwidth in Section 4.3 , with other Bayesian and non-Bayesian baselines. Code implementation is available at https://github.com/taotangtt/gp-manifold-regression .

# 5.1 Algorithm summary

For Bayesian inference, we marginalize out the unknown regression function f using conjugacy of the GP prior. This produces a posterior for the bandwidth parameter t , which we sample from using MetropolisHastings. With a GP prior, for a given bandwidth t , the conditional posterior of f is available analytically. We average over the samples from the marginal posterior of t in estimating the posterior mean ˆ f . Details of the algorithm are given in Appendix D.1 .

[Page 13]

Method We generate n training data samples { X i ,Y i } n i =1 , and compute out-of-sample error on a separate test data set. We use n = 50 , 100 , 200 , 400 , 800 , 1600 training samples, and compare performances of the following approaches:

- (i) Kernel ridge w/CV: Kernel ridge regression, where the kernel bandwidth is selected using a validation set consisting of 10% training data.
- (ii) GP rescaled Gamma: Bayesian regression with GP prior, where the kernel bandwidth t is sampled from the posterior of the rescaled Gamma prior by MCMC. Here we use the true manifold dimension d = 2 in the implementation.
- (iii) GP EB (ours): Bayesian regression with GP prior and the empirical Bayes (EB) prior p ( t ) in Section 4.3 .


On training samples (the in-sample case), we also implement another baseline where one uses the observed value Y i as the estimate of f ( X i ). Because this only uses information on one data sample, we call this baseline “single point”.

To further investigate alternative approaches, we also implemented (ii’) GP estimated d : a variant of (ii), where the manifold dimension is estimated from data as proposed in [ YD16 ], (iv) GP max-likelihood: selecting the kernel bandwidth t based on maximizing the marginal likelihood; (v) GP median heuristic: setting t to be the median of the distances between samples. We apply to the Swiss Roll data at sample size n = 50 , 100 , 200, and the results are detailed in Appendix D.3 . The proposed EB (iii) and (iv) are comparable and perform the best, with (ii’) similar on testing error and worse on training error, and (v) giving much larger errors. Further comparison of the distribution of the errors reveals that (ii’) can give long-tail outlier in-sample errors at small sample size, likely due to the unstable estimation of the manifold dimension; The proposed EB (iii) shows more stable performance in comparison.

Evaluation metrics We compute the in-sample error || ˆ f − f ∗ || n on the training set, and the out-of-sample error using n te = 5000 test samples that were not used at all in data fitting or hyperparameter choice. We compute the mean and standard deviation of || ˆ f − f ∗ || n ( || ˆ f − f ∗ || 2 ) on training (testing) data, respectively, over repeated experiments, and we repeat 200 runs when n ≤ 200, and 100 runs when n > 200.

Result The training and testing errors are plotted in Figure 1 (b)(c). The performances of the two GP methods (ii)(iii) are very close, such that the curves almost overlap in both plots. Recall that in (ii) we inserted the true intrinsic dimensionality (instead of estimating it from data as proposed in [ YD16 ]), while (iii) does not use such information. The result suggests that the proposed empirical Bayes prior adapts to the dimension well and obtains comparable performance in estimating f ∗ . The single-point method cannot generalize to test samples, and the in-sample performance is surpassed by other baselines when training size increases.

The two GP methods perform better than (i) kernel ridge with cross validation: (i) is slightly worse on the testing error, and the gap is more visible on the training error, where (i) also gives larger variance at small training size. Kernel ridge regression shares a form similar to the posterior mean in GP regression, and cross-validation is used to optimize its generalization performance with respect to squared error loss. Thus, (i) may have a potential advantage in terms of test error. We have observed in additional simulations (by adjusting the parameters of Swiss Roll data and f ∗ ) that (i) can perform better than GP methods on the out-of-sample error, while the in-sample error is still worse (results not reported). The larger variance in in-sample error by (i), particularly at small sample sizes, is likely due to (i) requiring a validation set and hence reduces the training set. An additional advantage of GP methods in practice lies in the potential ability to characterize uncertainty in the estimation of the regression function and prediction (not shown in this work).

Figure 2: Same plots as in Figure 1 for Swiss roll plus curve data.

Method and evaluation We follow the same procedure of creating training and testing sets, computing the baselines (i)(ii)(iii), and reporting training and testing errors as in Section 5.2 . When computing the rescaled Gamma GP (ii), we insert the intrinsic dimensionality d = 2. For (iii), we adopt the modified ˆ v n as in ( 157 ) in the proposed EB prior ( 12 ).

Result The mean and standard deviation of || ˆ f − f ∗ || n on training data and || ˆ f − f ∗ || 2 on testing data are shown in Figure 2 (b)(c) respectively. The relative performances of the different baselines are mostly similar to the case of the Swiss roll data in Figure 1 . The proposed GP model (iii) performs comparably to the GP baseline (ii); the kernel ridge (i) is comparable on the testing error and worse on the in-sample error, especially at small training size. We emphasize that though this dataset consists of a union of two manifolds having distinct dimensions, strictly speaking, it goes beyond the theoretical assumption of our extended theory in Appendix B.3 because the two manifolds intersect. The experimental result suggests that the proposed method can extend to more complex data of intrinsically low dimensionality.

# 5.4 Lucky Cat data

Data We study a dataset of high dimensional image data with intrinsic low dimensionality induced by a one-dimensional group action, following the setup in [ YD16 ]. The Lucky Cat dataset [ NNM + 96 ] contains 72 images of size 128 × 128, resulting in the ambient dimensionality being D = 16 , 384. Each sample (image) X i is the side view of a 3D object from a rotation angle θ i ∈ [0 , 2 π ], and thus the samples lie on a 1-dimensional manifold embedded in the high dimensional Euclidean space. Figure 3 (a) shows two examples of the image data. Because the image X in this dataset and the rotation angle θ have a one-to-one correspondence, we set f ∗ ( X ) = cos( θ ), which is a continuous function on the one-dimensional data manifold. The response Y i is as in ( 1 ) where σ = 0 . 1.

Method and evaluation For all three baselines (i)(ii)(iii), we randomly partition n = 18, 36, and 54 samples into a training set, leaving the remaining samples as a testing set. This process is repeated 400 times for each training size, and || ˆ f − f ∗ || 2 on the testing set is reported. We inserted the true intrinsic dimensionality d = 1 when computing the rescaled Gamma GP (ii). Not assuming known σ , we also implement the Bayes estimation of σ jointly with f using our EB prior (iii), where we adopt a prior of σ 2 uniformly on [10 − 4 , 1].

Result As shown in the table in Figure 3(b), the proposed GP model (iii) performs comparably to the GP model (ii), and both perform better than the (i) kernel ridge in the out-of-sample error. Note that (ii) presumes knowledge of the true intrinsic dimensionality, which, when the sample size is small, may be difficult to estimate from data. In the table, we also include two additional baselines, Lasso [Tib96] and Elastic net (EL-net) [ZH05], for reference. The mean and standard deviation of the errors of EL-net and Lasso are quoted from [YD16] (averaged over 100 repeated runs). Note that (iii) maintained comparable performance when inferring σ jointly with f . Overall, on this dataset where the sample size is very small (only a few tens) compared to the data dimensionality, the GP models outperform the other methods across all training sizes.

# 6 Discussion

The work can be extended in several future directions. It would be interesting to develop RKHS approximation analysis on a more general low-dimensional domain X . To do this, one will need to define the notion of smoothness s properly when intrinsically (non-linear) low-dimensional structures are present in data. It would also be helpful to improve the RKHS covering number analysis when restricted to a low-dimensional X , particularly, to improve the dependence on the ambient dimension D . Meanwhile, one can try to cover more complicated stratified space beyond the case of finite union of disjoint manifolds, e.g., an infinite union. In addition, it would be useful to extend our findings to other types of kernels, such as the Mat´ern kernel, and evaluate the theoretical and practical behaviors of these kernels. Finally, further comparison with more Gaussian process methods, in theory and in practical applications, would help to advance the understanding on this topic.

Empirical Bayes methods. It would be interesting to further compare and study the different empirical Bayes (EB) approaches. In particular, our EB prior based on the averaged kernel affinity ˆ v n ( t ) shows improved performance over the EB method based on estimating the manifold dimension at small sample size, yet the current asymptotic theory cannot explain the advantage. A fully non-asymptotic analysis focusing on finite sample size would be helpful. Another potentially fruitful direction is to extend our analysis to more EB methods, e.g., the GP MLE approach, which we numerically studied. The theoretical analysis of the MLE approach has its own challenge [ KO23 ] and needs to be under a different framework. We expect that some of our estimates, such as the manifold RKHS approximation results in Section 4 , will be transferable to the analysis of general Bayesian and non-Bayesian kernel methods.

Observation variance. In this work, we have assumed known σ in our posterior contraction rate analysis. The theory may be extended to infer unknown σ (by choosing a prior on σ ) following the arguments in [ vdVvZ08b , vdVvZ09 ] based on the general framework in [ GvdV07 , GGvdV00 ]. This corresponds to a full Bayesian approach. It would also be useful to extend the theory to allow estimating σ by certain empirical Bayes methods. Meanwhile, our analysis suggests that the constant C in our convergence rate scales with the ratio σ/ ∥ f ∗ ∥ (Remark A.1 ), though the expression is only an upper bound. One can interpret ∥ f ∗ ∥ 2 /σ 2 as a Signal-to-Noise Ratio, as hinted by the information theoretical arguments in [ vdVvZ11 ]. We think information theoretical techniques, possibly combined with a non-asymptotic analysis, can help to further elucidate the influence of σ 2 on the nonparametric Bayesian approach.

# 7.2 Proof of f j ∈ C k − 2 j,β ( M ) and Lemma 4.1 (ii)

Recall the definition of f j ( x ) in ( 30 ), and we have shown that f 0 = f . Thus, the statement ∥ f j ∥ k − 2 j,β ≤ ˜ C 2 ∥ f ∥ k,β trivially holds when j = 0 with constant ˜ C 2 = 1.

To prove the cases for j ≥ 1, we will need to analyze the differential property of f j on the manifold. Using the symbol S i 1 ,ℓ,i 2 ,i 3 ( x ) defined in ( 31 ), we have the equivalent expression of f j as in ( 32 ), so we focus on the differential property of S i 1 ,ℓ,i 2 ,i 3 . Recall that a combination of indices { i 1 ,ℓ,i 2 ,i 3 } that contribute to the summation in ( 32 ), which we call a valid combination, must satisfy ( 36 ). Since ⌊ k/ 2 ⌋ ≥ j ≥ 1, we consider k ≥ 2.

Strictly speaking, the expression ( 31 ) stands for the value of S i 1 ,ℓ,i 2 ,i 3 at a point x only, where θ is unit vector in T x M . When x moves on M , the tangent plane T x M also changes, and thus the formal definition of S i 1 ,ℓ,i 2 ,i 3 ( x ) as a function of x should be

$$
S _ { i _ { 1 } , \ell , i _ { 2 } , i _ { 3 } } ( x ) = \int _ { S _ { x } ^ { d - 1 } } A _ { i _ { 1 } , \ell } ( x , \theta ) V _ { i _ { 3 } } ( x , \theta ) \nabla _ { \theta } ^ { i _ { 2 } } f ( x ) d \theta ,
$$

where S d − 1 x is the unit ( d − 1)-sphere in T x M . To analyze the covariant derivative of S i 1 ,ℓ,i 2 ,i 3 ( x ), we will introduce a parallel frame which provides a differentiable mapping T ( y,θ ) that maps from every y (in a neighborhood of x ) and θ ∈ S d − 1 , the unit ( d − 1)-sphere in R d , to a unit vector in T y M . Using this mapping, we will show that

$$
S _ { i _ { 1 } , \ell _ { i _ { 2 } , i _ { 3 } } ( y ) } = \int _ { S ^ { d - 1 } } A _ { i _ { 1 } , \ell } ( y , T ( y , \theta ) ) V _ { i _ { 3 } } ( y , T ( y , \theta ) ) \nabla _ { T ( y , \theta ) } ^ { i _ { 2 } } f ( y ) d \theta , \ \forall y \in B _ { \xi } ( x ) ,
$$

# • Parallel frame and the covariant derivatives of S i 1 ,ℓ,i 2 ,i 3 ( x )

We introduce the parallel frame {E i } d i =1 defined on B ξ ( x ) ⊂ M : For any y ∈ B ξ ( x ), recall that P x,y : T x M → T y M denote the parallel transport from x to y ; Let { E i } d i =1 be an orthonormal basis of T x M , and we define E i ( y ) = P x,y E i , i = 1 , ··· ,d . At x , this gives that E i ( x ) = E i . As a result, {E i ( y ) } d i =1 form an orthonormal basis of T y M . Meanwhile, because E i is parallel along each radial geodesic, for any v ∈ S d − 1 x ⊂ T x M , we have

$$
\nabla _ { \dot { \gamma } } \mathcal { E } _ { i } = 0 \quad \text {along the geodesic } \gamma ( t ) = \exp _ { x } ( t v ) , \, | t | < \xi .
$$

With the parallel frame, we define the mapping T ( y,θ ) as

$$
( y , \theta = ( u _ { 1 } , \cdots , u _ { d } ) ) \mapsto T ( y , \theta ) = \sum _ { i = 1 } ^ { d } u _ { i } \mathcal { E } _ { i } ( y ) \in T _ { y } \mathcal { M } , \ \forall y \in B _ { \xi } ( x ) , \, \theta \in \mathbb { S } ^ { d - 1 } .
$$

The mapping T ( y, · ) : S d − 1 → S d − 1 y preserves the measure on S d − 1 , because at any y , {E i ( y ) } d i =1 form an orthonormal basis. Evaluating ( 38 ) at any y ∈ B ξ ( x ), we then have ( 39 ) hold by change of variable of θ .

The usage of ( 39 ) lies in that we now have an expression of S i 1 ,ℓ,i 2 ,i 3 on a neighborhood B ξ ( x ) of x . The idea to compute and analyze the covariance derivative of S i 1 ,ℓ,i 2 ,i 3 is by leveraging a “tensor-field view” of the integrand on the r.h.s. of ( 39 ). Specifically, for each fixed θ ∈ S d − 1 , we define U θ ( y ) := T ( y,θ ) and then U θ is a vector field on B ξ ( x ). We will show that ∀ y ∈ B ξ ( x ),

$$
A _ { i _ { 1 } , \ell } ( y , T ( y , \theta ) ) V _ { i _ { 3 } } ( y , T ( y , \theta ) ) \nabla _ { T ( y , \theta ) } ^ { i _ { 2 } } f ( y ) = ( \bar { A } _ { i _ { 1 } , \ell } \bar { V } _ { i _ { 3 } } \bar { F } _ { i _ { 2 } } ) ( \underbrace { U _ { \theta } , \cdots , U _ { \theta } } _ { i _ { 1 } + i _ { 2 } + i _ { 3 } \text { many} } ) | _ { y } ,
$$

where ¯ A i 1 ,ℓ , ¯ V i 3 , and ¯ F i 2 are tensor fields on M of order i 1 , i 3 , and i 2 respectively, satisfying

$$
A _ { i _ { 1 } , \ell } ( y , U _ { \theta } ( y ) ) = \bar { A } _ { i _ { 1 } , \ell } ( U _ { \theta } , \cdots , U _ { \theta } ) | _ { y } ,
$$

$$
V _ { i _ { 3 } } ( y , U _ { \theta } ( y ) ) = \bar { V } _ { i _ { 3 } } ( U _ { \theta } , \cdots , U _ { \theta } ) | _ { y } ,
$$

$$
\nabla _ { U _ { \theta } ( y ) } ^ { i _ { 2 } } f ( y ) = \bar { F } _ { i _ { 2 } } ( U _ { \theta } , \cdots , U _ { \theta } ) | _ { y } .
$$

The construction of ¯ F i 2 is direct by the covariant derivative of f : we let ¯ F i 2 = ∇ i 2 f which is an orderi 2 tensor field. For any vector field U on M , we have ¯ F i 2 ( U, ··· ,U ) | y = ∇ i 2 U ( y ) f ( y ) for any y ∈ M by definition of the covariant derivative. Thus we have ( 44 ) hold for all 0 ≤ i 2 ≤ k , and we also have that

$$

The construction of ¯ A i 1 ,ℓ and ¯ V i 3 are results of Lemma C.12 . We first consider ¯ V i 3 . By Lemma C.12 (i) b), when i 3 ≥ 2, there exists an orderi 3 tensor field ¯ V i 3 on M s.t., for any vector field U on M , ¯ V i 3 ( U, ··· ,U ) | y = ¯ V i 3 ( y )( U ( y ) , ··· ,U ( y )) = V i 3 ( y,U ( y )), ∀ y ∈ M . The tensor field ¯ V i 3 consists of sums of products of the curvature tensor and its covariant derivatives up to ( i 3 − 2)-th order, including a contraction of the tensors. When i 3 = 0 or 1, we set ¯ V 0 = 1 and ¯ V 1 = 0 which are constant tensor fields. We then have ( 43 ) hold for all 0 ≤ i 3 ≤ k , and ∇ m ¯ V i 3 is a tensor field determined by the curvature tensor and its covariant derivatives up to max { m + i 3 − 2 , 0 } -th order. ¯

To construct A i 1 ,ℓ , recall the definition of A i 1 ,ℓ in ( 22 ) for i 1 ≥ 4 and ℓ ≥ 1. For 4 ≤ i 1 ≤ 2 k and 1 ≤ l ≤ ⌊ i 1 / 4 ⌋ , we define ¯ A i 1 ,ℓ :=   j 1 + ··· + j ℓ = i 1 4 ≤ j 1 , ··· ,j ℓ ¯ q j 1 ··· ¯ q j ℓ , where, applying Lemma C.12 (ii) b), each ¯ q j i is an orderj i tensor field on M s.t. for any vector field U on M , ¯ q j i ( U, ··· ,U ) | y = ¯ q j i ( y )( U ( y ) , ··· ,U ( y )) = q j i ( y,U ( y )), ∀ y ∈ M . By now our construction fulfills ( 42 )( 43 )( 44 ), then by definition of U θ we have ( 41 ) hold. where we used that j ≥ 1 in the last inequality. Then, by the expression of f j as in ( 32 ) and triangle inequality,

Define ˜ C 2 ( M ,d,k ) := max { 1 , ˜ c ( k,d ) k 3 3 k max { diam ( M ) , 1 }| S d − 1 | ¯ C A ¯ C V } finishes the proof of statement (ii). The constants ¯ C A and ¯ C V depend on the manifold curvature tensor and II and their covariant derivatives, as described above ( 51 ). Thus the constant dependence of ˜ C 2 ( M ,d,k ) is as declared in the lemma.

Finally, we collect the needed regularity of the manifold. In the proof of statement (i), when k ≥ 2, we need up to ( k − 2)-th (continuous) covariant derivatives of curvature tensor and up to ( k + 1)-th intrinsic derivatives of the Riemann metric tensor g , as well as up to (2 k − 2)-th (continuous) covariant derivatives of II; when k = 0 , 1 we need 2nd intrinsic derivatives of g and continuous 1st covariant derivative of II. In the proof of statement (ii), we only need regularity of M when k ≥ 2: up to ( k − 1)-th continuous covariant derivatives of curvature tensor and again up to (2 k − 3)-th continuous covariant derivatives of II. Overall, it suffices to have max { 2 k, 3 } regularity of M , that is, M is C max { 2 k, 3 } .

Remark 2 (More general h ) . The proof only uses the differentiability and decay property of the function h ( r ), and thus can be extended beyond when h ( r ) = e − r/ 2 – specifically, Assumption A.3 (i) suffices. To extend the proof, first truncate the 2 δ ( ϵ ) geodesic ball by letting δ ( ϵ ) =   1 2 a ( d + k + 1) ϵ log( 1 ϵ ), which will ensure that when y ∈ M \ B 2 δ ( ϵ ) ( x ), h   ∥ ι ( x ) − ι ( y ) ∥ 2 R D /ϵ   ≤ a 0 ϵ ( d + k +1) / 2 by that | h ( r ) | ≤ a 0 e − ar . We define h a ( r ) := e − ar , then | h ( l ) ( r ) | ≤ a l h a ( r ), and h a is a monotonically decay function. In all the bounding of the remainder terms, we replace h ( u 2 ) to be h a ( u 2 ), and h ( u 2 / 4) to be h a ( u 2 / 4), multiplied by a constant depending on k which is max 0 ≤ ℓ ≤ max { k, 1 } a ℓ , and we use h a ( u 2 ) ≤ h a ( u 2 / 4) by monotonicity of h a . All the additional k -dependent constant (including the dependence on { a ℓ ,ℓ ≤ k } ) multiplied to upper bounds can be absorbed into the constants in front, and we have additional dependence on a . Meanwhile, we keep “ h ( ℓ ) ( u 2 )” in the definition of f j (and subsequently in R (4) j ( x )) and in the definition of the moments M ( ℓ ) i :=   ∞ 0 h ( ℓ ) ( u 2 ) (2 π ) d/ 2 u i du , removing the ( − 2) ℓ factor in the denominator. In bounding | R (4) j ( x ) | , we apply Lemma C.15 with a change of variable u  → √ 2 au , resulting in a factor of max { 1 , (2 a ) − k − d/ 2 } multiplied to the constant c ( k,d ). Thus the constant C R, 4 also depends on a . All this will give the same bounds of the remainder terms as in Lemma 4.1 (i) where the constants C R, 2 , C R, 3 , C R, 4 are modified to absorb the additional factors depending on a , d , k . In the proof of Lemma 4.1 (ii), the analysis of S i 1 ,ℓ,i 2 ,i 3 is not affected; In the last step when we bound ∥ f j ∥ k − 2 j,β from ∥ S i 1 ,ℓ,i 2 ,i 3 ∥ k − 2 j,β , we use | M ( ℓ ) i | ≤   ∞ 0 a ℓ h a ( u 2 ) (2 π ) d/ 2 u i du in the upper bound, the summation of which can be bounded by a constant depending on k , d , and a . This again will give the same bounds as before, where the constants are modified in its dependence on a , d , and k .

# A Proofs in Section 3 and extension

The proofs in Section 3 follow the framework in [ vdVvZ09 ] and [ YD16 ]. For convergence rate of posterior mean estimator under fixed design (Theorem 3.2 ), we avoid the need of function truncation and improve from the result in [ YD16 ].

[Page 33]

# A.1 Fixed design results

The posterior contraction rate under hierarchical GP priors ( 3 ) was studied in [ vdVvZ09 ] and in [ YD16 ] for manifold data, under the general framework of [ GGvdV00 , GvdV07 ]. Following these previous works, our strategy to prove the posterior contraction rate is to verify a set of conditions on our hierarchical GP prior ( 3 ). Specifically, suppose we can specify two sequences ε n and ¯ ε n , which are asymptotically o (1) as n → ∞ , and we can show that for some Borel measurable subsets B n of C ( X ) and n sufficiently large, the following three inequalities hold

$$
P [ | | f ^ { t } - f ^ { * } | | _ { \infty } \leq \varepsilon _ { n } ] \geq e ^ { - n \varepsilon _ { n } ^ { 2 } } ,
$$

$$
P [ f ^ { t } \notin B _ { n } ] \leq e ^ { - 4 n \varepsilon _ { n } ^ { 2 } } ,
$$

$$

$$

then the posterior contraction rate with respect to || · || n would be at least ε n ∨ ¯ ε n , up to multiplying an absolute constant. In ( 59 )( 60 ) and below, the notation P stands for the prior Π and we adopt this convention in our proof following the literature.

Proof for Lemma A.1 . First, we prove (ii) in the random design case. We recall the definition of ε n ,r n ,N n ,δ n , and B n = B N n ,r n ,δ n ,ε n from the proof of Theorem 3.1 . We have already shown in ( 60 )( 61 ) that

$$

As a consequence, by inserting our definition of B n in the proof of Theorem 2.1 in [ GGvdV00 ] and following their steps, we obtain a sequence of measurable sets A n such that Pr ( n ) ( A c n ) → 0, and for constant c 5 > 0, E ( n ) ( 1 A n Π( || f t − f ∗ || ≥ ¯ ε n |{ X i ,Y i } n i =1 )) ≤ e − c 5 nε 2 n . This proves the case (ii).

The proof for (i) in the fixed design case is by adapting the above argument from [ GGvdV00 ] to the fixed design case. Specifically, though that theorem focused on i.i.d. observations, its proof can be adapted to independent but not identically distributed observations. The extension to regression with fixed-design follows by applying the techniques in [ GvdV07 ].

and below we bound ∥ 1 ○ ∥ n , ∥ 2 ○ ∥ n and ∥ 3 ○ ∥ n respectively.

Because f ∗ is bounded, let ∥ f ∗ ∥ ∞ ≤ M for some positive constant M . The bounds for ∥ 1 ○ ∥ n and ∥ 3 ○ ∥ n are straightforward:

$$

and thus, by ( 92 ), we have that with large n ,

To bound ∥ 2 ○ ∥ n , first observe that

$$
\| \mathfrak { O } \| _ { n } & \leq \int \| f ^ { t } \| _ { n } 1 _ { \{ \| f ^ { t } - f ^ { * } \| _ { n } \geq \varepsilon _ { n } \} } d \Pi ( f ^ { t } | X , Y ) \\ & \leq \left ( \int \| f ^ { t } \| _ { n } ^ { 2 } d \Pi ( f ^ { t } | X , Y ) \right ) ^ { 1 / 2 } \Pi ( \| f ^ { t } - f ^ { * } \| _ { n } \geq \bar { \varepsilon } _ { n } | X , Y ) ^ { 1 / 2 } ,

# A.2 Random design results

To prove the random design results, we need the following lemma for comparing || · || n and || · || 2 based on empirical process theory [ Gee00 ]. Let H B ( ε, F , || · || ) denote the ε -bracketing entropy of a function space F with respect to a norm || · || .

Lemma A.2 (Lemma 5.16 in [ Gee00 ]) . Suppose X i are i.i.d drawn from a distribution, and denote by Pr ( n ) X the joint law of { X i } n i =1 . If for some ¯ M > 0 , the function class F satisfies that sup f ∈F || f || ∞ ≤ ¯ M , ω > 0 satisfies that n ¯ M − 2 ω 2 ≥ H B ( ¯ M − 1 ω, F , || · || 2 ) , and η ∈ (0 , 1) , then there exists C 5 > 0 such that

$$
\Pr _ { X } ^ { ( n ) } \left [ \sup _ { f \in \mathcal { F } , | | f | | _ { 2 } \geq 3 2 \omega / \eta } \left | \frac { | | f | | _ { n } } { | | f | | _ { 2 } } - 1 \right | \geq \eta \right ] \leq 8 \exp \{ - C _ { 5 } n \bar { M } ^ { - 2 } \omega ^ { 2 } \eta ^ { 2 } \} .
$$

We are ready to prove Theorem 3.3 using Lemma A.1 (ii) and Lemma A.2 .

# A.3 Adaptive rate with mis-specified prior

In this section, we show that when the intrinsic dimension is not known, under some conditions, we can still obtain posterior contraction but potentially with a sub-optimal rate. We first modify Assumption 2 (A3) on the prior to (A3’).

Assumption A.1. (A3’) Given positive constants ϱ and s , there exist positive constants c 1 , c 2 , c 3 , a 1 , a 2 , K 1 , K 2 , C 1 , C 2 , and ϱ + ≥ ϱ − > 0 , such that ϱ + ≥ ϱ and

$$
p ( t ) & \geq C _ { 1 } t ^ { - a _ { 1 } } \exp \left ( - \, \frac { K _ { 1 } } { t ^ { \frac { 2 } { 2 } / 2 } } \right ) \, f o r \, t \in [ c _ { 1 } n ^ { \frac { - 2 } { 2 s + \theta _ { + } } } ( \log n ) ^ { \frac { 2 ( 1 + D ) } { 2 s + \theta _ { + } } } , c _ { 2 } n ^ { \frac { - 2 } { 2 s + \theta _ { + } } } ( \log n ) ^ { \frac { 2 ( 1 + D ) } { 2 s + \theta _ { + } } } ] , \\ p ( t ) & \leq C _ { 2 } t ^ { - a _ { 2 } } \exp \left ( - \, \frac { K _ { 2 } } { t ^ { \theta - \, / 2 } } \right ) \, f o r \, t \in ( 0 , c _ { 3 } n ^ { \frac { - 2 \theta _ { + } } { 2 s + \theta _ { + } } } ( \log n ) ^ { \frac { - 4 ( 1 + D ) } { 2 ^ { + \theta / s ) \theta _ { - } } } ] .
$$

The condition ϱ + ≥ ϱ − ensures that, when n is large enough, the two intervals for the lower and upper bounds of p ( t ) to hold will not overlap, and thus the required lower and upper bounds can be satisfied at the same time.

$$
r ( \varrho , \varrho _ { \pm } , s ) = \frac { 1 } { 2 } \left ( 1 - \frac { \varrho _ { + } } { ( \rho _ { - } \wedge \varrho ) } \frac { \varrho } { ( 2 s + \varrho _ { + } ) } \right ) ,

with k as above for the two cases respectively.

This theorem suggests that the posterior can adapt to the low dimensional structure of X even when we put a prior on the bandwidth not dependent on the intrinsic dimension. The rate n − r ( ϱ,ϱ ± ,s ) is slower than the rate with known dimension ϱ , which is n − s/ (2 s + ϱ ) = n − 1 2 (1 − ϱ 2 s + ϱ ) . The rate n − r ( ϱ,ϱ ± ,s ) gets better when ϱ + and ϱ − get closer, and when ϱ − = ϱ + = ϱ , we recover the rate n − s/ (2 s + ϱ ) as has been proved in Section 3.3 . Larger s and smaller ϱ + impose less constraints on the feasible ϱ − to prevent the rate n − r ( ϱ,ϱ ± ,s ) from degenerating. Illustration of the feasible region and the change of r ( ϱ,ϱ ± ,s ) with ϱ ± are shown in Figure A.1 .
