Proof of Corollary 4.3 . We want to apply Theorems 3.1 , 3.2 and 3.3 to prove the corollary. Since Assumption 2 (A3) is already satisfied with s = k + β and ϱ = d , it suffices to verify that Assumption 1 is satisfied with the same s and ϱ .

First, (A1) holds with ϱ = d because X = M is a d -dimensional manifold, see Example 3.1 . Meanwhile, (A2) is satisfied as a result of Proposition 4.2 . Specifically, let constants ϵ 2 ( M ), γ 1 ( M ,d,k ), γ 2 ( M ,d,k ) be as in Proposition 4.2 . We set

$$
\nu _ { 1 } = \gamma _ { 1 } ( \mathcal { M } , d , k ) \| f ^ { * } \| _ { k , \beta } , \ \nu _ { 2 } = \gamma _ { 2 } ( \mathcal { M } , d , k ) \| f ^ { * } \| _ { k , \beta } ^ { 2 } .
$$

When ϵ < ϵ 2 ( M ) set to be ϵ 0 , applying Proposition 4.2 with f = f ∗ , we have the two bounds ( 10 ) and ( 11 ) hold with the function G ϵ ( F ). This allows to use G ϵ ( F ) as the needed F ϵ ∈ H ϵ ( M ) in (A2) to approximate f ∗ , and the two bounds imply ( 5 ). Thus, we have shown that (A2) holds with s = k + β and ϱ = d .

Consequently, the fixed-design result follows from Theorems 3.1 and 3.2 , and the random-design result follows from Theorem 3.3 .

Remark A.1 (Dependence on σ ) . To reveal how the convergence rate depends on the noise level σ , observe that (assuming σ is known and fixed) one can rescale Y i by dividing by σ , thereby considering the σ = 1 case and replacing f ∗ with f ∗ /σ in the analysis. Substituting into our definitions of the constants ¯ C 1 and ¯ C 2 in the proof of Theorem 3.1 , we note that only ν 1 = γ 1 ∥ f ∗ ∥ k,β and ν 2 = γ 2 ∥ f ∗ ∥ 2 k,β depend on f ∗ (which is f ∗ /σ ). In particular, ¯ C 2 does not involve f ∗ . This will lead to ∥ f − f ∗ ∥ n ≤ Cn − s 2 s + ϱ (log n ) k 1 + k 2 in the posterior contract rate where the dependence of C on f ∗ and σ can be explicitly tracked. Specifically, one can show that C ≤ ∥ f ∗ ∥ max { C ′ 1 ,C ′ 2 σ ∥ f ∗ ∥ ,C ′ 2 (1 + σ 2 ∥ f ∗ ∥ 2 ) 1 / 2 } ≤ C ′ ∥ f ∗ ∥ (1 + σ 2 ∥ f ∗ ∥ 2 ) 1 / 2 , where ∥ f ∗ ∥ = ∥ f ∗ ∥ k,β and the constants C ′ 1 , C ′ 2 , C ′ 3 and C ′ do not involve f ∗ or σ .

# B.3 Extension to finite union of disjoint manifolds

Assumption A.2 (Finite union of manifolds) . The data domain X = ∪ m i =1 M i , where each M i is a d i dimensional smooth connected closed Riemannian manifolds isometrically embedded in [0 , 1] D ⊂ R D , and the m manifolds are mutually disjoint. Let ¯ d = max 1 ≤ i ≤ m d i .

We denote by ι : ∪ m i =1 M i → R D be the isometric embedding of X = ∪ m i =1 M i in [0 , 1] D . Because the m manifolds are disjoint, we have

$$
\mathfrak { m } _ { 0 } = \min _ { i \neq j } \min _ { x \in \mathcal { M } _ { i } , y \in \mathcal { M } _ { j } } \| \iota ( x ) - \iota ( y ) \| _ { \mathbb { R } ^ { D } } > 0 .
$$

̸

If we allow X to change when the sample size n increases, our analysis directly extends when m 0 is O (1), meaning uniformly bounded away from zero, see the proof of Lemma B.4 below. This assumption on the separation between M i can be further relaxed by considering small ϵ depending on n , and we postpone such extension for exposition simplicity.

Our definition of H¨lder class on manifold (Definition 1 ) also naturally extends to the case of finite union of manifolds. Specifically, under Assumption A.2 , for k = 0 , 1 , ··· , 0 < β ≤ 1, we say f ∈ C k,β ( X ) if f | M i ∈ C k,β ( M i ) for each i , and we define ∥ f ∥ k,β = max 1 ≤ i ≤ m ∥ f | M i ∥ k,β .

The integral operator G ϵ previously defined in ( 8 ) is now taking the integral over the union of the m manifolds. Specifically, suppose that f ∈ L 1 ( X ), i.e. f | M i ∈ L 1 ( M i ) for all i , and let dV i be the volume form of M i . For any x ∈ X , we define

$$
G _ { \epsilon } ( f ) ( x ) = \sum _ { i = 1 } ^ { m } \int _ { \mathcal { M } _ { i } } \frac { 1 } { ( 2 \pi \epsilon ) ^ { d _ { i } / 2 } } h \left ( \frac { \| \iota ( x ) - \iota ( y ) \| _ { \mathbb { R } ^ { D } } ^ { 2 } } { \epsilon } \right ) f ( y ) d V _ { i } ( y ) .
$$

To verify Assumption 1 (A1), we say that a set S satisfies (A1) with dimension d if there exists C S and r 0 such that ( 4 ) holds with ϱ = d . Here, “dimension” of S is not unique, since for any d ′ > d , S also satisfies (A1) with dimension d ′ . Applying Lemma B.7 m times, we have that X satisfies (A1) with dimension ¯ d . Assumption 1 (A2) can be verified similarly as in the proof of Corollary 4.3 based on Proposition B.5 .

Lemma B.7. Suppose X 1 , X 2 ⊂ [0 , 1] D satisfy Assumption 1 (A1) with dimensions ϱ 1 and ϱ 2 respectively, then X = X 1 ∪ X 2 satisfies (A1) with dimension ϱ = max { ϱ 1 ,ϱ 2 } .

Proof. By definition, there exist 0 < r 1 < 1, 0 < r 2 < 1, and C X 1 > 0 and C X 2 > 0 such that N ( r, X 1 , ∥·∥ ∞ ) ≤ C X 1 r − ϱ 1 for all 0 < r ≤ r 1 and N ( r, X 2 , ∥ · ∥ ∞ ) ≤ C X 2 r − ϱ 2 for all 0 < r ≤ r 2 . Since the union of any covers of X 1 and X 2 is a cover of X = X 1 ∪ X 2 , we have that N ( r, X , ∥ · ∥ ∞ ) ≤ N ( r, X 1 , ∥ · ∥ ∞ ) + N ( r, X 2 , ∥ · ∥ ∞ ) always holds. Hence, for all 0 < r ≤ r 0 := min { r 1 ,r 2 } ∈ (0 , 1], we have

$$
\mathcal { N } ( r , \mathcal { X } , \| \cdot \| _ { \infty } ) & \leq \mathcal { N } ( r , \mathcal { X } _ { 1 } , \| \cdot \| _ { \infty } ) + \mathcal { N } ( r , \mathcal { X } _ { 2 } , \| \cdot \| _ { \infty } ) \leq C _ { \mathcal { X } _ { 1 } } r ^ { - \varrho _ { 1 } } + C _ { \mathcal { X } _ { 2 } } r ^ { - \varrho _ { 2 } } \\ & \leq ( C _ { \mathcal { X } _ { 1 } } + C _ { \mathcal { X } _ { 2 } } ) r ^ { - \max \{ e _ { 1 } , e _ { 2 } \} } ,
$$

and this proves the claim with ϱ = max { ϱ 1 ,ϱ 2 } .

# C Technical lemmas and proofs

# C.1 RKHS lemmas

# C.1.1 Lemmas about RKHS on a subset of R D

We consider a general class of h to be specified in Assumption A.3 below. Because h ϵ ( x,x ′ ) is the covariance function of the Gaussian process F ϵ x , when ϵ = 1, there exists a finite measure dµ on R D , namely the spectral measure of F 1 , such that

$$
h _ { 1 } ( x , x ^ { \prime } ) = h ( \| x - x ^ { \prime } \| ^ { 2 } ) = \int _ { \mathbb { R } ^ { D } } e ^ { - i \lambda ^ { T } ( x - x ^ { \prime } ) } d \mu ( \lambda ) .
$$

In particular, we have For other values of ϵ > 0, the spectral measure is dµ ϵ , and µ = µ 1 . We have

$$
h ( 0 ) = h _ { 1 } ( x , x ) = \int _ { \mathbb { R } ^ { D } } d \mu ( \lambda ) > 0 \text { and is finite.}
$$

[Page 60]

$$
h _ { \epsilon } ( x , x ^ { \prime } ) = \int _ { \mathbb { R } ^ { D } } e ^ { - i \lambda ^ { T } ( x - x ^ { \prime } ) } d \mu _ { \epsilon } ( \lambda ) = \int _ { \mathbb { R } ^ { D } } e ^ { - i \lambda ^ { T } ( x - x ^ { \prime } ) / \sqrt { \epsilon } } d \mu ( \lambda ) .
$$

In our setting, the spectral measure will have a density, denoted as f ϵ , namely, dµ ϵ ( λ ) = f ϵ ( λ ) dλ . By change of variable, for any ϵ > 0, f ( λ ) = ϵ D/ 2 f ( √ ϵλ ) . (159)

$$
f _ { \epsilon } ( \lambda ) = \epsilon ^ { D / 2 } f _ { 1 } ( \sqrt { \epsilon } \lambda ) .
$$

In addition, because h 1 ( x,x ′ ) = h ( ∥ x − x ′ ∥ 2 ) is radial symmetric, then so is f 1 ( λ ) as the Fourier transform of the function h ( ∥ x ∥ 2 ), that is, f 1 ( λ ) = f 1 , r ( ∥ λ ∥ ) for some positive function f 1 , r on [0 , ∞ ).

Assumption A.3 (General kernel function) . The kernel h ϵ ( x,x ′ ) = h ( ∥ x − x ′ ∥ 2 /ϵ ) where h : [0 , ∞ ) → R is associated with the spectral measure dµ and satisfies that

(i) Differentiability and decay of h . h ∈ C [0 , ∞ ) ∩ C ∞ (0 , ∞ ) and there exist a,a l > 0 s.t.

$$
| h ^ { ( l ) } ( r ) | \leq a _ { l } e ^ { - a r } , \ \forall r \geq 0 , \forall l = 0 , 1 , \cdots .
$$

h (0) > 0 and without loss of generality we assume h (0) = 1 .

(ii) Spectral measure has subexponential decay. There exist δ h > 0 and c h > 1 s.t.

$$
\int _ { \mathbb { R } ^ { D } } e ^ { \delta _ { h } \| \lambda \| } d \mu ( \lambda ) \leq c _ { h } ^ { 2 D } , \ \forall D = 1 , 2 , \cdots ,
$$

namely the constants δ h and c h are uniform for all D .

(iii) The spectral measure dµ has density f 1 , and f 1 has monotonic radial decay, i.e., for any λ ∈ R D and any a ≥ 1 , f 1 ( aλ ) ≤ f 1 ( λ ) .

In Assumption A.3 , (i) is to ensure the extension of Lemma 4.1 used in the RKHS approximation analysis, see Remark 2 . The proof only uses up to ( ⌊ k/ 2 ⌋ + 1)-th derivative of h ( r ), and thus the C ∞ of h can be relaxed to be C ⌊ k/ 2 ⌋ +1 , that is, to “match” that of f ∗ . Assumption A.3 (ii)(iii) are by following the setup in [ vdVvZ09 ] to enable a series technical estimates of the RKHS. The assumption covers the squared exponential kernel in ( 2 ), corresponding to h ( r ) = e − r/ 2 , as a special case: (i) is satisfied by letting a l = 1, a = 1 / 2; With h ( r ) = e − r/ 2 , h 1 ( x,x ′ ) is a Gaussian kernel in R D , and its spectral measure also has a density that is Gaussian on R D . Then (iii) holds, and (ii) holds with δ h = 1 / 2 and c h = √ 2.

The next three lemmas largely follow the arguments in [ YD16 , vdVvZ09 ], and we prove under our Assumption A.3 . With such general h ϵ , let H ϵ ( X ) and H ϵ ([0 , 1] D ) be the RKHS associated with the kernel h ϵ on X and [0 , 1] D respectively. The first lemma characterizes the relationship between the two.

Lemma C.1. Suppose h ϵ satisfies Assumption A.3 . Given X ⊂ [0 , 1] D , ¯ D

- (i) For any f ∈ H ϵ ( X ) , there exists a unique f ∈ H ϵ ([0 , 1] ) such that ¯ f | X = f and || ¯ f || H ϵ ([0 , 1] D ) = || f || H ϵ ( X ) . Moreover, for any g ∈ H ϵ ([0 , 1] D ) with g | X = f , it holds that || g || H ϵ ([0 , 1] D ) ≥ || f || H ϵ ( X ) , and “ = ” is achieved only when g = ¯ f in H ϵ ([0 , 1] D ) . D
- (ii) For any g ∈ H ϵ ([0 , 1] ) , g | X ∈ H ϵ ( X ) .


Proof of Lemma C.1 . The lemma follows [ YD16 , Lemma 5.1] which assumed square-exponential kernel and that is a submanifold, but the argument extends here. D

To prove (i), we construct an isometry Φ between H ϵ ( X ) and a complete subspace of H ϵ ([0 , 1] ) such that Φ maps every member in

$$

$$

By that Φ is an isometry and again ∥ h ϵ ( x, · ) ∥ H ϵ ([0 , 1] D ) = h ϵ ( x,x ) 1 / 2 = 1, one can similarly show that Φ ( f n ) converges to Φ ( f ) pointwisely on [0 , 1] D . Combined with that Φ ( f n ) | X = f n by the definition of Φ on ˜ H , we have that that Φ ( f ) | X = f . D ¯ ¯

Now consider any g ∈ H ϵ ([0 , 1] ) satisfying g | X = f . By that ( g − f ) | X = 0, we have that g − f is in the orthogonal complement of Φ ( H ϵ ( X )) in H ϵ ([0 , 1] D ), then

by the Pythagorean theorem. This means that || g || H ϵ ([0 , 1] D ) ≥ || ¯ f || H ϵ ([0 , 1] D ) and “=” is only achieved when g = ¯ f in H ϵ ([0 , 1] D ). This also proves that ¯ f = Φ ( f ) is the unique extension of f in H ϵ ([0 , 1] D ) such that preserves the RKHS norm of f in H ϵ ( X ).

To prove (ii), again by the orthogonal decomposition H ϵ ([0 , 1] D ) = Φ ( H ϵ ( X )) ⊕ Φ ( H ϵ ( X )) ⊥ , there exists h ∈ H ϵ ( X ) such that g − Φ ( h ) ∈ Φ ( H ϵ ( X )) ⊥ . This means that

Lemma C.4. Suppose h ϵ satisfies Assumption A.3 , for any ϵ > 0 , H ϵ ([0 , 1] D ) consists of real parts of the functions

Proof of Lemma C.4 . The lemma follows [ vdVvZ09 , Lemma 4.1] applied to domain [0 , 1] D ⊂ R D . To show the statement of f = h ψ , recall that the proof of [ vdVvZ09 , Lemma 4.1] is by first letting ψ run though the complex-number linear span L of the sets of functions { e x ( λ ) = e − iλ T x , x ∈ [0 , 1] D } and then take the L 2 ( µ ϵ ) closure ¯ L of L . There is a mapping P from L to its subset L , which consists of real-number linear span of { e x } , by taking the real-part of the coefficients in the linear combination. One can verify that ∀ φ ∈ L , h P R φ = Re h φ . We then extend P R to ¯ L = L 2 ( µ ϵ ) by taking the closure, then ∀ φ ∈ L 2 ( µ ϵ ), Re h φ = h P R φ . Thus, using the first part of the lemma we have f = Re h φ for a φ ∈ L 2 ( µ ϵ ), and letting ψ = P R φ gives that f = h ψ and is real-valued. Finally, to show that ∥ f ∥ H ϵ ([0 , 1] D ) = ∥ ψ ∥ L 2 ( µ ϵ ) , first verify that ∥ h ψ ∥ H ϵ ([0 , 1] D ) = ∥ ψ ∥ L 2 ( µ ϵ ) for any ψ ∈ L R , and this means that the mapping H : ψ  → h ψ is an isometry from L R to H ( L R ). By taking the closure ¯ L R of L R in L 2 ( µ ϵ ), H is also an isometry on ¯ L R . Lemma 8.1]. Specifically, for any f ∈ H 1 ϵ 1 ( X ), by Lemma C.1 (i), there exists ¯ f ∈ H ϵ 1 ([0 , 1] D ) s.t. ¯ f | X = f and ∥ ¯ f ∥ H ϵ 1 ([0 , 1] D ) = ∥ f ∥ H ϵ 1 ( X ) ≤ 1. We also introduce a lemma to compute the RKHS norm of functions expressed as kernel integral operator applied to another function.

Lemma C.5. Suppose h ϵ satisfies Assumption A.3 , given X ⊂ [0 , 1] D , for any ϵ > 0 , let H ϵ ( X ) be the RKHS associated with h ϵ . Let dν be a measure on X , suppose h ϵ ( · ,y ) is in L 2 ( X ,dν ) for any y ∈ [0 , 1] D , and   X   X h ϵ ( x,y ) 2 dν ( x ) dν ( y ) < ∞ . It is possible to prove the conclusion in even more general settings. In this work, we apply Lemma C.5 to when ( X ,dx ) = ( M ,dV ) or when X is a finite union of disjoint manifolds (Assumption A.2 ), when X is always a subset of [0 , 1] D . In our usage, the needed integrability conditions by Lemma C.5 are always satisfied because h ϵ is continuous under Assumption A.3 and ι ( M ) is continuous and compact domain.

This also gives that   X f ( x ) 2 dν ( x ) ≤ ∥ g ∥ 2 L 2 ( X ,dν )   X   X k ( x,y ) 2 dν ( y ) dν ( x ) < ∞ , and thus f ∈ L 2 ( X ,dν ).

$$

$$

by the producing property of k , i.e., ⟨ k ( · ,y ) ,k ( · ,y ′ ) ⟩ ˜ H = k ( y,y ′ ). We now show that the r.h.s. is finite: by definition of f and Cauchy-Schwarz,

$$

$$

because both g and f are in L 2 ( X ,dν ). It remains to show that f is in ˜ H to finish the proof. D

To do this, we will first show that f (as a function on [0 , 1] ) is in the RKHS H = H ϵ ([0 , 1] D ), then f | X is in ˜ H by Lemma C.1 (ii).

Because h ϵ ( x,y ) is real-valued, using the spectral measure representation, we have

$$
h _ { \epsilon } ( x , y ) = \int _ { \mathbb { R } ^ { D } } e ^ { - i \lambda ^ { T } ( x - y ) } d \mu _ { \epsilon } ( \lambda ) = \int _ { \mathbb { R } ^ { D } } e ^ { i \lambda ^ { T } ( x - y ) } d \mu _ { \epsilon } ( \lambda ) .

$$
\int _ { \mathbb { R } ^ { D } } | \psi ( \lambda ) | ^ { 2 } d \mu _ { \epsilon } ( \lambda ) & = \int _ { \mathbb { R } ^ { D } } \int _ { \chi } \int _ { \chi } e ^ { - \lambda \chi ^ { T } ( y - y ^ { \prime } ) } g ( y ) g ( y ^ { \prime } ) d \nu ( y ) d \nu ( y ^ { \prime } ) d \mu _ { \epsilon } ( \lambda ) \\ & = \int _ { \chi } \int _ { \chi } h _ { \epsilon } ( y , y ^ { \prime } ) g ( y ) g ( y ^ { \prime } ) d \nu ( y ) d \nu ( y ^ { \prime } ) ,

# C.1.2 RKHS covering lemma and small ball probability on a general subset

These important estimates are used in the proofs in Section 3 .

$$
\log \mathcal { N } ( \varepsilon ^ { \prime } , \mathbb { H } _ { t } ^ { 1 } ( \mathcal { X } ) , | | \cdot | | _ { \infty } ) \leq K _ { 1 } t ^ { - \varrho / 2 } ( \log \frac { c ^ { D } } { \varepsilon ^ { \prime } } ) ^ { D + 1 } , \ \forall 0 < \varepsilon ^ { \prime } < 1 ,
$$

and N ( ε ′ , H 1 t ( X ) , || · || ∞ ) = 1 when ε ′ ≥ 1 . The constant c depends on µ , and K 1 depends on µ , D and X . In particular, there is K > 1 which depends on µ , D and X , s.t.

$$

$$

The proof adopts techniques from Lemma 4.5 in [ vdVvZ09 ], and when we construct a net to cover the domain X we invoke Assumption 1 (A1) and bring in the factor ϱ in the scaling.

Proof of Lemma C.6 . We apply the following result from [ HS21 , Corollary A.8]: For all t, ε ′ > 0, we have

$$
\log \mathcal { N } ( \varepsilon ^ { \prime } , \mathbb { H } _ { t } ^ { 1 } ( \mathcal { X } ) , | | \cdot | | _ { \infty } ) \leq \mathcal { N } ( \sqrt { t } , \mathcal { X } , | | \cdot | | _ { \infty } ) \log \mathcal { N } ( \varepsilon ^ { \prime } , \mathbb { H } _ { 1 } ^ { 1 } ( [ - 1 , 1 ] ^ { D } ) , | | \cdot | | _ { \infty } ) ,
$$

where in the covering number of X , ∥·∥ ∞ is in R D , and in the covering numbers of RKHS balls, ∥·∥ ∞ stands for the functional infinity norm on the corresponding domains. By Assumption 1 (A1), when 0 < t < r 2 0 , N ( √ t, X , || · || ∞ ) ≤ C X t − ϱ/ 2 . Hence, we need to bound log N ( ε ′ , H 1 1 ([ − 1 , 1] D ) , || · || ∞ ).

Lemma C.8. Suppose X ⊂ [0 , 1] D satisfies Assumption 1 (A1). For t ≤ 1 , under Assumption A.3 about the kernel h , consider the Gaussian process f t on X associated with h t . Let s t = E [ ∥ f t ∥ 2 ∞ | t ] 1 / 2 . Then, s t ≤ cDt − 1 / 2 , where c > 1 is a constant depending a 1 Assumption A.3 (i) on h .

The proof of Lemma C.8 uses similar techniques as Lemma 5 in [ CR24 ], and we include the detailed proof for completeness. Given a general topological space S , denote by W y , y ∈ S , a GP on S . Let ∥ W ∥ ∞ := sup y ∈ S | W y | . Recall the Borell-TIS and Dudley’s inequality for GP, see e.g. Chapter 2 of [ GN21 ].

Lemma C.9 (Borell–TIS, Theorem 2.5.8 [ GN21 ]) . Suppose W y , y ∈ S , is a centered GP where Pr[ ∥ W ∥ ∞ < ∞ ] > 0 . Let σ 2 = sup y ∈ S E W 2 y . Then, ∀ u ≥ 0 ,

$$
\Pr [ \| W \| _ { \infty } - \mathbb { E } \| W \| _ { \infty } \geq u ] \leq e ^ { - u ^ { 2 } / 2 \sigma ^ { 2 } } , \quad \Pr [ \| W \| _ { \infty } - \mathbb { E } \| W \| _ { \infty } \leq - u ] \leq e ^ { - u ^ { 2 } / 2 \sigma ^ { 2 } } .
$$

Lemma C.10 (Dudley, Theorem 2.3.7 [ GN21 ]) . Define a metric d ( y,y ′ ) 2 := E | W y − W y ′ | 2 on S , and let 2 σ 0 = sup y,y ′ d ( y,y ′ ) . Then, for any y 0 ∈ S ,

$$
\mathbb { E } \| W \| _ { \infty } \leq \mathbb { E } | W _ { y _ { 0 } } | + 4 \sqrt { 2 } \int _ { 0 } ^ { \sigma _ { 0 } } \sqrt { 2 \log \mathcal { N } ( \varepsilon , S , d ) } d \varepsilon .
$$

[Page 69]

Proof of Lemma C.8 . Denote by V the variance of a random variable. By that

$$
s _ { t } ^ { 2 } = \mathbb { E } [ \| f ^ { t } \| _ { \infty } | t ] ^ { 2 } + \mathbb { V } [ \| f ^ { t } \| _ { \infty } | t ] ,
$$

we bound each term in the above expression. Since E [ f t ( x ) 2 | t ] = h t ( x,x ) = h (0) = 1 for any x ∈ X , we can apply Lemma C.9 with σ 2 = 1 to obtain

$$
\Pr \{ | | f ^ { t } | | _ { \infty } - \mathbb { E } | | f ^ { t } | | _ { \infty } | \geq u | t \} \leq 2 e ^ { - u ^ { 2 } / 2 } .
$$

# C.2 Differential geometry lemmas

# C.2.1 Riemannian geometry concepts

Given the metric tensor g , the manifold (Riemannian) distance between two points x and y on M is the infimum of the lengths of all piece-wise regular curves on M connecting x and y . Because M is connected and compact, for any two points x,y on M there exists a length-minimizing geodesic joining from x to y , and the length of the geodesic is equal to the manifold distance. We call this distance the geodesic distance and denote it by d M ( x,y ). In this case, ( M ,d M ) is a complete metric space. Meanwhile, g induces a (local) Riemannian volume form on M , denoted by dV , and ( M ,dV ) is a measure space. We call V ol ( M ) :=   M dV the volume of M .

We consider the geodesic ball centered at a point x ∈ M that is diffeomorphic to a Euclidean ball in R d . This is characterized by the exponential map at x when the radius of the ball is less than the injectivity radius of M , denoted as ξ > 0. ξ = min x ∈M inj ( x ), where inj ( x ) is the injectivity radius at point x . For any x ∈ M , let T x M ∼ = R d denote the tangent space of M at x . Let B r ( x ) ⊂ M denote the open geodesic ball of radius r centered at x , and B R d r ( u ) the open ball in R d of radius r and center u . Then

$$
\exp _ { x } \colon B _ { \xi } ^ { \mathbb { R } ^ { d } } ( 0 ) \subset \mathbb { R } ^ { d } \cong T _ { x } \mathcal { M } \to B _ { \xi } ( x ) \subset \mathcal { M }
$$

[Page 70]

is a diffeomorphism, and the corresponding coordinates are called normal coordinates at x .

The normal coordinate is closely related to the geodesic curve. For any y ∈ B ξ ( x ), there is a unique (constant speed) minimizing geodesic γ from x to y , satisfying γ (0) = x and γ ( t ) = y where t = d M ( x,y ) < ξ . This curve can be extended to be defined on t ∈ ( − ξ,ξ ), and along the curve, ˙ γ ( t ) ∈ T γ ( t ) M and ∥ ˙ γ ( t ) ∥ ≡ 1 where the norm is induced by g at γ ( t ). At t = 0, ˙ γ (0) = v ∈ S d − 1 ⊂ T x M , and γ can be expressed as γ ( t ) = exp x ( tv ).

The ℓ -th covariant derivative of f is an orderℓ tensor field on M . We introduce the notations for vector fields and tensor fields on M . We say that U is a vector field on M if U ( x ) ∈ T x M for every x ∈ M , and usually we consider U having the same order of differentiability as M , e.g., when M is smooth then U is also smooth. We can also consider a vector field on a neighborhood on M . For an orderr tensor field T ( r ) on M (or a neighborhood of M ), the evaluation T ( r ) at a point x gives a tensor T ( r ) ( x ) : T x M × ··· T x M        r many → R .

For vector fields U 1 , ··· ,U r on M , we use the notation T ( r ) ( U 1 , ··· ,U r ) | x = T ( r ) ( x )( U 1 ( x ) , ··· ,U r ( x )).

The first covariant derivative ∇ f is an order-1 tensor field and can be specified by “directional derivative”: at any x ∈ M and ∀ v ∈ T x M , we denote ∇ f ( x )( v ) also as ∇ v f ( x ) := d dt f ( γ ( t )) | t =0 , where γ ( t ) is a differentiable curve on M s.t. γ (0) = x and γ ′ (0) = v . By definition, for a vector field U , ∇ f ( U ) | x = ∇ f ( x )( U ( x )) = ∇ U ( x ) f ( x ). We also write ∇ f ( U ) as ∇ U f . Given ℓ -th derivative of f , the ℓ + 1-derivative is defined as

$$
\nabla ^ { \ell + 1 } f ( V , U _ { 1 } , \dots , U _ { \ell } ) & = ( \nabla _ { V } \nabla ^ { \ell } f ) ( U _ { 1 } , \dots , U _ { \ell } ) \\ & = \nabla _ { V } [ \nabla ^ { \ell } f ( U _ { 1 } , \dots , U _ { \ell } ) ] - \nabla ^ { \ell } f ( \nabla _ { V } U _ { 1 } , \dots , U _ { \ell } ) - \dots - \nabla ^ { \ell } f ( U _ { 1 } , \dots , \nabla _ { V } U _ { \ell } ) ,
$$

where V and U 1 ,...,U ℓ are arbitrary vector fields on M . For a general differentiable tensor field T ( r ) and vector fields V , U 1 ,...,U r , we have

$$
\nabla T ^ { ( r ) } ( V , U _ { 1 } , \cdots , U _ { r } ) & = ( \nabla _ { V } T ^ { ( r ) } ) ( U _ { 1 } , \cdots , U _ { r } ) \\ & = \nabla _ { V } [ T ^ { ( r ) } ( U _ { 1 } , \cdots , U _ { r } ) ] - T ^ { ( r ) } ( \nabla _ { V } U _ { 1 } , \cdots , U _ { r } ) - \cdots - T ^ { ( r ) } ( U _ { 1 } , \cdots , \nabla _ { V } U _ { r } ) .
$$

The definition of ∇ ℓ +1 f is same as the covariant derivative of the tensor field ∇ ℓ f .

# C.2.2 Local expansions of volume form and Euclidean distance

We recall the notations as in Section 2.4 . In particular, M is a d -dimensional connected close (compact and without boundary) Riemannian manifold isometrically embedded in R D through ι : M → R D . We say M is C r with r ≥ 1 integer when both the Riemannian metric g and the embedding map ι are at least C r . The injectivity radius of M is ξ , and the reach of ι ( M ) is τ .

We first introduce a lemma on the comparison of Euclidean distance and manifold geodesic distance.

Lemma C.11 (Proposition 6.3 in [ NSW08 ]) . Suppose M is a C 2 manifold isometrically embedded in R D with reach τ > 0 , then for any two points x,y ∈ M with ∥ ι ( x ) − ι ( y ) ∥ < τ/ 2 , we have d M ( x,y ) ≤ 2 ∥ ι ( x ) − ι ( y ) ∥ .

Proof of Lemma C.11 . It was shown in [ NSW08 , Proposition 6.3] that for any two points x,y ∈ M with ∥ ι ( x ) − ι ( y ) ∥ < τ/ 2, we always have d M ( x,y ) ≤ τ − τ   1 − 2 ∥ ι ( x ) − ι ( y ) ∥ /τ . The statement of the lemma then follows from that 1 − √ 1 − x ≤ x for all x ∈ [0 , 1].

The local expansions of volume form and Euclidean distance at a point x ∈ M have been derived in the literature, see e.g. [ Gra74 , WW18 ]. We recall these expansions in the first few terms: below, ( t,θ ) is the polar coordinate in T x M ∼ = R d . We have

$$
d V ( \exp _ { x } ( t ) ) & = \left ( 1 - \frac { 1 } { 6 } R i c _ { x } ( \theta , \theta ) t ^ { 2 } - \frac { 1 } { 1 2 } \nabla _ { \theta } R i c _ { x } ( \theta , \theta ) t ^ { 3 } \\ & - \left ( \frac { 1 } { 4 0 } \nabla _ { \theta } ^ { 2 } R i c _ { x } ( \theta , \theta ) + \frac { 1 } { 1 8 0 } \sum _ { a , b = 1 } ^ { d } R _ { x } ( \theta , E _ { a } , \theta , E _ { b } ) ^ { 2 } - \frac { 1 } { 7 2 } R i c _ { x } ( \theta , \theta ) ^ { 2 } ) t ^ { 4 } \\ & + O ( t ^ { 5 } ) \right ) t ^ { d - 1 } d t d \theta ,
$$

[Page 71]

where R x and Ric x are the curvature tensor and the Ricci curvature tensor of M at x respectively, and { E a } d a =1 is an orthonormal basis of T x M ;

$$
\| \iota \circ \exp _ { x } ( t \theta ) - \iota ( x ) \| _ { \mathbb { R } ^ { D } } ^ { 2 } & = t ^ { 2 } - \frac { 1 } { 1 2 } \| \mathbb { I } _ { x } ( \theta , \theta ) \| ^ { 2 } t ^ { 4 } - \frac { 1 } { 1 2 } \nabla _ { \theta } \mathbb { I } _ { x } ( \theta , \theta ) \cdot \mathbb { I } _ { x } ( \theta , \theta ) t ^ { 5 } \\ & - ( \frac { 1 } { 4 0 } \nabla _ { \theta } ^ { 2 } \mathbb { I } _ { x } ( \theta , \theta ) \cdot \mathbb { I } _ { x } ( \theta , \theta ) + \frac { 1 } { 4 5 } \nabla _ { \theta } \mathbb { I } _ { x } ( \theta , \theta ) \cdot \nabla _ { \theta } \mathbb { I } _ { x } ( \theta , \theta ) ) t ^ { 6 } + O ( t ^ { 7 } ) ,
$$

where II x is the second fundamental form of ι ( M ) at ι ( x ).

These expansions, however, are not enough for our purpose because in this work we will need to expand to arbitrary high order depending on k the target function differentiability order. Meanwhile, we do not use the specific expressions of the expansion and can treat each O ( t ℓ ) term as abstract. An important observation is that in the above expansions, the terms that involve θ and multiplied to t ℓ are tensor fields evaluated at x and ( θ, ··· ,θ ) ∈ T x M × ··· × T x M , and that is why the t ℓ factor can be extracted thanks to the linearity of tensor fields. For our analysis, we will need to show that this pattern holds for expansion to arbitrarily high orders; we also need to show that the constant in big-O is uniform for x and reveal the constant dependence. These needed results are summarized in the following lemma, for which we include a proof for completeness.

Lemma C.12. For any x ∈ M , we consider the normal coordinates at x provided by exp x : T x M ∼ = R d → M , and on R d we use the polar coordinates ( t,θ ) .

(i) Local expansion of volume form.

a) Suppose M is C 2 , then ∀ 0 ≤ t < ξ , θ ∈ S d − 1 ⊂ T x M ,

$$
M \ \ i s \ C ^ { 2 } , \ t h e n \ \forall 0 \leq t < \xi , \, \theta \in S ^ { d - 1 } \subset T _ { x } \mathcal { M } , \\ d V ( \exp _ { x } ( t \theta ) ) = ( 1 + R _ { V } ( t ) ) t ^ { d - 1 } d t d \theta , \quad | R _ { V } ( t ) | \leq C _ { V , 1 } t ^ { 2 } ,
$$

where the constant C V, 1 depends on d and the uniform bounds of up to the 2nd intrinsic derivative of the Riemannian metric g , and C V, 1 is uniform for x ∈ M .

b) Given K ≥ 2 , suppose M is C K +1 . For each ℓ = 2 , ··· , K , there exist an orderℓ tensor field ¯ V ℓ on M such that, after defining V ℓ ( x,v ) := ¯ V ℓ ( x )( v, ··· ,v ) for any v ∈ T x M , we have ∀ 0 ≤ t < ξ , θ ∈ S d − 1 ⊂ T x M ,

$$
d V ( \exp _ { x } ( t \theta ) ) = ( 1 + \sum _ { \ell = 2 } ^ { \kappa } V _ { \ell } ( x , \theta ) t ^ { \ell } + R _ { V , \kappa } ( t ) ) t ^ { d - 1 } d t d \theta , \quad | R _ { V , \kappa } ( t ) | \leq C _ { V , \kappa } t ^ { \kappa + 1 } ,
$$

where the constant C V, K depends on d and the uniform bounds of the intrinsic derivatives of g up to ( K +1) -th order, and C V, K is uniform for x ∈ M . In addition, ¯ V ℓ can be expressed through the products and sums of the curvature tensor R of M and its covariant derivatives up to ( ℓ − 2 )-th order, including a contraction of the tensors, with coefficients depending on d and ℓ .

(ii) Local expansion of squared Euclidean distance.

a) Suppose M is C 3 , then ∀ 0 ≤ t < min { 1 ,ξ } , θ ∈ S d − 1 ⊂ T x M ,

$$
\| \iota \circ \exp _ { x } ( t \theta ) - \iota ( x ) \| _ { \mathbb { R } ^ { D } } ^ { 2 } = t ^ { 2 } + R _ { q } ( t ) , \ \ | R _ { q } ( t ) | \leq c _ { q , 3 } t ^ { 4 } ,
$$

where the constant c q, 3 depends on the ∥ · ∥ ∞ norm of the second fundamental form II of ι ( M ) and its first covariant derivative, and c q, 3 is uniform for x ∈ M .

b) Given J ≥ 4 , suppose M is C J . For each j = 4 , ··· , J , there exist an orderj tensor field ¯ q j on M such that, after defining q j ( x,v ) := ¯ q j ( x )( v, ··· ,v ) for any v ∈ T x M , we have ∀ 0 ≤ t < min { 1 ,ξ } , θ ∈ S d − 1 ⊂ T x M ,

$$
\| \iota \circ \exp _ { x } ( t \theta ) - \iota ( x ) \| _ { \mathbb { R } ^ { D } } ^ { 2 } = t ^ { 2 } + \sum _ { j = 4 } ^ { \mathcal { J } } q _ { j } ( x , \theta ) t ^ { j } + R _ { q , \mathcal { J } } ( t ) , \quad | R _ { q , \mathcal { J } } ( t ) | \leq c _ { q , \mathcal { J } } t ^ { \mathcal { J } + 1 } ,
$$

where the constant c q, J depends on the ∥ · ∥ ∞ norm of the up to ( J − 2 )-th covariant derivatives of II , and c q, J is uniform for x ∈ M . In addition, ¯ q j can be expressed through the dot products and sums of the second fundamental form II of ι ( M ) and its covariant derivatives up to ( j − 4 )-th order with coefficients depending on j .

The tensor D m det[˜ g ](0) will be of central importance for our analysis, and we will show that it can be characterized by the curvature tensor on M (and its covariant derivatives). # C.2.3 Taylor expansion of manifold H¨lder function and a lemma on tensor field

Lemma C.13. Given f ∈ C k,β ( M ) for non-negative integer k and 0 < β ≤ 1 , for any x ∈ M , any θ ∈ S d − 1 and t < ξ , we have

$$
f ( \exp _ { x } ( t \theta ) ) = \sum _ { i = 0 } ^ { k } \frac { t ^ { i } } { i ! } \nabla _ { \theta } ^ { i } f ( x ) + r _ { f } ( t ) , \ \ | r _ { f } ( t ) | \leq \frac { 1 } { k ! } L _ { k , \beta } ( f , x ) t ^ { k + \beta } .
$$

Remark A.2 . In the bound of the remainder term, the factor L k,β ( f,x ) ≤ L k,β ( f ) ≤ ∥ f ∥ k,β . The lemma derives a Taylor expansion of f but it differs from the Taylor expansion in normal coordinates of f , namely that of f ◦ exp x as a function on R d . Specifically, the proof uses the fact that along the geodesic γ ( t ) = exp x ( tθ ) from x , the t -derivatives of the function f ◦ γ can always be interpreted as covariant derivatives of f at γ ( t ) in the direction of ˙ γ , due to that ˙ γ is parallel along γ . In contrast, the partial derivatives of f ◦ exp x in R d usually do not equal the covariant derivatives of f unless it is at the origin.

Proof of Lemma C.13 . For fixed x , θ , we use the geodesic γ ( t ) s.t. γ (0) = x , ˙ γ (0) = θ , and then we consider F ( t ) := f (exp x ( tθ )) = f ( γ ( t )) as a one-dimensional function of t . For any l = 0 , ··· ,k and | t | < ξ , we have

$$
F ^ { ( l ) } ( t ) = \nabla ^ { l } f ( \gamma ( t ) ) ( \dot { \gamma } ( t ) , \cdots , \dot { \gamma } ( t ) ) , \ \dot { \gamma } ( t ) = P _ { x , \gamma ( t ) } \theta ,
$$

where P x,y : T x M → T y M is the parallel transport. In particular, F ( l ) (0) = ∇ l f ( x )( θ, ··· ,θ ). By Taylor expansion of F ( t ) at t = 0 up to ( k − 1)-th derivative, we have

$$
f ( \exp _ { x } ( t \theta ) ) = F ( t ) = \sum _ { i = 0 } ^ { k - 1 } \frac { t ^ { i } } { i ! } \nabla _ { \theta } ^ { i } f ( x ) + \frac { t ^ { k } } { k ! } \nabla _ { P _ { x , \gamma ( s ) } \theta } ^ { k } f ( \gamma ( s ) ) , \quad \text {for some } s \in [ 0 , t ] .
$$

By the definition of L k,β ( f,x ), we have that

$$
| \nabla _ { P _ { x , \gamma ( s ) } } ^ { k } f ( \gamma ( s ) ) - \nabla _ { \theta } ^ { k } f ( x ) | \leq L _ { k , \beta } ( f , x ) s ^ { \beta } \leq L _ { k , \beta } ( f , x ) t ^ { \beta } .
$$

[Page 76]

This gives that

$$
f ( \exp _ { x } ( t \theta ) ) = \sum _ { i = 0 } ^ { k - 1 } \frac { t ^ { i } } { i ! } \nabla ^ { i } _ { \theta } f ( x ) + \frac { t ^ { k } } { k ! } ( \nabla ^ { k } _ { \theta } f ( x ) + \tilde { r } ( t ) ) , \ \ | \tilde { r } ( t ) | \leq L _ { k , \beta } ( f , x ) t ^ { \beta } ,
$$

which proves the lemma.

Lemma C.14. Suppose T ( r ) is an C p +1 orderr tensor field on M , r ≥ 0 , p ≥ 0 , and recall the condensed notation ( 47 ) . Then, for any x ∈ M ,

$$
\sup _ { y \in B _ { \xi } ( x ) } \sup _ { v , \theta \in S _ { x } ^ { d - 1 } } | \nabla _ { v } ^ { p } T ^ { ( r ) } ( x ) ( \theta ) - \nabla _ { P _ { \varepsilon , v } } ^ { p } T ^ { ( r ) } ( y ) ( P _ { x , y } \theta ) | \leq M _ { p + 1 } d _ { \mathcal { M } } ( x , y ) ,
$$

where the constant M p +1 is defined as

$$
M _ { p + 1 } \colon = \sup _ { x \in \mathcal { M } } \sup _ { v , \theta \in S _ { x } ^ { d - 1 } } | \nabla ^ { p + 1 } T ^ { ( r ) } ( x ) ( \underbrace { v , \cdots , v } _ { p + 1 \ m a n y } , \underbrace { \theta , \cdots , \theta } _ { r \ m a n y } ) | .
$$

Proof of Lemma C.14 . For any x ∈ M and any y ∈ B ξ ( x ), we consider the radial geodesic γ ( t ) = exp x ( tw ) from x to y s.t. γ ( s ) = y , s = d M ( x,y ), γ (0) = x , and ˙ γ (0) = w ∈ S d − 1 x . Given any v, θ ∈ S d − 1 x fixed, we can define two parallel vector fields U and V along the geodesic γ ( t ) for | t | ≤ ξ as

$$
V ( \gamma ( t ) ) = P _ { x , \gamma ( t ) } v , \ U ( \gamma ( t ) ) = P _ { x , \gamma ( t ) } \theta ,
$$

and then we have

$$
\nabla _ { \dot { \gamma } } V = 0 , \ \ \nabla _ { \dot { \gamma } } U = 0 , \ \ \ a l o n g \ t h e \ g e o d e s i c \ \gamma ( t ) , \, | t | < \xi .
$$

We consider the function

$$
H ( t ) \coloneqq \nabla _ { P _ { x , \gamma ( t ) } } ^ { p } T ^ { ( r ) } ( \gamma ( t ) ) ( P _ { x , \gamma ( t ) } \theta ) = \nabla ^ { p } T ^ { ( r ) } ( \underbrace { V , \cdots , V } _ { p \text { many} } , \underbrace { U , \cdots , U } _ { r \text { many} } ) | _ { \gamma ( t ) } ,
$$

and we have

$$
H ( 0 ) = \nabla _ { v } ^ { p } T ^ { ( r ) } ( x ) ( \theta ) , \ H ( s ) = \nabla _ { P _ { x , y } v } ^ { p } T ^ { ( r ) } ( y ) ( P _ { x , y } \theta ) .
$$

By Mean Value Theorem, H ( s ) − H (0) = sH ′ ( t ) at some t ∈ [0 ,s ], and observe that

$$
H ^ { \prime } ( t ) = \nabla ^ { p + 1 } T ^ { ( r ) } ( \gamma ( t ) ) ( \dot { \gamma } ( t ) , \underbrace { V ( \gamma ( t ) ) , \cdots , V ( \gamma ( t ) ) } _ { p \text { many} } , \underbrace { U ( \gamma ( t ) ) , \cdots , U ( \gamma ( t ) ) } _ { r \text { many} } )
$$

because V and U are parallel along γ . To bound | H ′ ( t ) | , we let γ ( t ) = z , and for each fixed θ ∈ S d − 1 z we consider the tensor

$$
T _ { \theta } ( w _ { 1 } , \cdots , w _ { p + 1 } ) \colon = \nabla ^ { p + 1 } T ^ { ( r ) } ( z ) ( w _ { 1 } , \cdots , w _ { p + 1 } , \underbrace { \theta , \cdots , \theta } _ { r \text { many} } ) , \ \ w _ { 1 } , \cdots , w _ { p + 1 } \in S _ { z } ^ { d - 1 } .
$$

Because T ( r ) is C p +1 on M , T θ is a symmetric tensor of order p + 1 (under normal coordinates), and then by Banach’s Theorem (see Section 2.4 ) we have | T θ ( w 1 , ··· ,w p +1 ) | ≤ sup v ∈ S d − 1 z | T θ ( v, ··· ,v ) | , namely, we have that ∀ θ ∈ S d − 1 z ,

$$
| \nabla ^ { p + 1 } T ^ { ( r ) } ( z ) ( w _ { 1 } , \cdots , w _ { p + 1 } , \underbrace { \theta , \cdots , \theta } _ { r \text { many} } ) | \leq \sup _ { v \in S _ { z } ^ { d - 1 } } | \nabla ^ { p + 1 } T ^ { ( r ) } ( z ) ( \underbrace { v , \cdots , v } _ { p + 1 \text { many} } , \underbrace { \theta , \cdots , \theta } _ { r \text { many} } ) | .
$$

Back to the expression of H ′ ( t ), since ˙ γ ( t ) , V ( γ ( t )) , U ( γ ( t )) ∈ S d − 1 z , we have

$$
| H ^ { \prime } ( t ) | & \leq \sup _ { w , v , \theta \in S _ { z } ^ { d - 1 } } | \nabla ^ { p + 1 } T ^ { ( r ) } ( z ) ( \underbrace { w , \underbrace { v , \cdots , v } _ { p \text { many } } \theta , \cdots , \theta } ) | \\ & \leq \sup _ { v , \theta \in S _ { z } ^ { d - 1 } } | \nabla ^ { p + 1 } T ^ { ( r ) } ( z ) ( \underbrace { v , \cdots , v } _ { p + 1 \text { many } } \theta , \cdots , \theta ) | \leq M _ { p + 1 } .
$$

This proves that |∇ p v T ( r ) ( x )( θ ) − ∇ p P x,y v T ( r ) ( y )( P x,y θ ) | = | H (0) − H ( s ) | ≤ sM p +1 .

The following lemma is used in the proof of Lemma 4.1 .

Lemma C.15. Suppose d,k ∈ Z , d ≥ 1 , k ≥ 0 . If 0 < ϵ < 1 /e , then there exists a constant c ( k,d ) only depending on k and d such that

$$
\int _ { 2 \sqrt { ( d + k + 1 ) \log ( \frac { 1 } { \epsilon } ) } } ^ { \infty } e ^ { - t ^ { 2 } / 2 } t ^ { i } d t \leq c ( k , d ) \epsilon ^ { d + k + 1 } , \ \forall 0 \leq i \leq 2 k + d - 1 .
$$

Putting together, we have

$$
e ^ { - t ^ { 2 } / 2 } t ^ { 2 k + d - 1 } \leq c ( k , d ) e ^ { - t ^ { 2 } / 4 } \frac { t } { 2 } , \ \forall t \in ( 2 , \infty ) .
$$

$$
\int _ { t _ { 0 } } ^ { \infty } e ^ { - t ^ { 2 } / 2 } t ^ { i } d t & \leq \int _ { t _ { 0 } } ^ { \infty } e ^ { - t ^ { 2 } / 2 } t ^ { 2 k + d - 1 } d t \leq \frac { c ( k , d ) } { 2 } \int _ { t _ { 0 } } ^ { \infty } e ^ { - t ^ { 2 } / 4 } t d t = c ( k , d ) \epsilon ^ { d + k + 1 } ,
$$

where in the last inequality we used that   ∞ t 0 e − t 2 / 4 tdt = 2 e − t 2 0 / 4 = 2 ϵ d + k +1 .

.

We also recall the classical Bernstein inequality used in the proof of Lemma B.1 .

Lemma C.16 (Bernstein inequality) . Let ξ j be i.i.d bounded random variables, j = 1 , ··· ,N . E ( ξ j ) = 0 , | ξ j | ≤ L and E ξ 2 j ≤ ν for positive constants L and ν . Then, ∀ τ > 0 ,

$$
\Pr \left [ \frac { 1 } { N } \sum _ { j = 1 } ^ { N } \xi _ { j } > \tau \right ] , \Pr \left [ \frac { 1 } { N } \sum _ { j = 1 } ^ { N } \xi _ { j } < - \tau \right ] \leq \exp \{ - \frac { \tau ^ { 2 } N } { 2 ( \nu + \frac { \tau L } { 3 } ) } \} .
$$

In particular, when τL < 3 ν , both the tail probabilities are bounded by exp {− 1 4 Nτ 2 ν } .

# D Experimental details

# D.1 Algorithm

Denote the observation data as X = { X i } n i =1 , Y = { Y i } n i =1 , and we are also given a stand-alone test set X te = { X te j } n te j =1 . We also denote by X the n -byD matrix, where each row is a sample X i ∈ R D . Similarly, Y is a lengthn vector, and X te is an n te -byD matrix. Given a kernel bandwidth t , we denote by h t ( X,X ) the n -byn kernel matrix, the i,j -th entry of which equals h t ( X i ,X j ). Similarly, h t ( X,X te j ) is a lengthn vector whose i -th entry equals h t ( X i ,X te j ).

Following the proposed method in Section 4.3 , we use the prior p ( t ) as defined in ( 12 )( 13 )( 14 ), where k = ⌈ 0 . 25log 2 ( n ) ⌉ and when n < 200 we set k = 2. The statistic T n is computed by averaging on a random subset S , where we choose | S | = ⌈ (log n ) 3 ⌉ and | S | = n when n < 200. We sample t from the marginal posterior which can be written as

$$
p ( t | X , Y ) \circ L ( Y | X , t ) p ( t ) ,
$$

where L ( Y | X,t ) is the marginal log-likelihood, and

$$
& \log ( L ( Y | X , t ) ) = \log ( P ( Y | X , t ) ) \\ & = - \frac { 1 } { 2 } Y ^ { T } ( h _ { t } ( X , X ) + \sigma ^ { 2 } I ) ^ { - 1 } Y - \frac { 1 } { 2 } \log ( | h _ { t } ( X , X ) + \sigma ^ { 2 } I | ) - \frac { n } { 2 } \log ( 2 \pi ) .
$$

In ( 180 ), we do not need to obtain the normalizing constant in p ( t ) because the Metropolis-Hasting MCMC only needs the ratio of the marginal posterior.

[Page 78]

Algorithm 1 Bayesian posterior-mean estimator (output on a test set)

Input : hyperparameters σ 2 ,a 0 ,b 0 , observed data X = { X i } n i =1 , Y = { Y i } n i =1 , test set X te = { X te j } n te j =1 , number of iterations B .

Output : { ˆ n

f ( X te j ) } te j =1 on the test set

- 1: Set initial value t 0 .
- 2: for b = 1 , ··· ,B do
- 3: Sample t b ∼ t | X,Y by Metropolis-Hasting MCMC from the marginal posterior ( 180 ), where L ( Y | X,t ) is as in ( 181 ), and p ( t ) is the EB prior as in ( 12 )( 13 )( 14 ).
- 4: Compute the conditional posterior mean ˆ f b ( X te j | t b ) = E [ f t b ( X te j ) | X,Y ] by ( 182 ) for j = 1 , ··· ,n te .
- 5: end for
- 6: Compute ˆ f ( X te j ) = 1 B   B b =1 ˆ f b ( X te j | t b ) for j = 1 , ··· ,n te .

Given a bandwidth t , we can compute

$$
\hat { f } ( X _ { j } ^ { t e } | t ) \colon = \mathbb { E } [ f ^ { t } ( X _ { j } ^ { t e } ) | X , Y ] = Y ^ { T } ( h _ { t } ( X , X ) + \sigma ^ { 2 } I ) ^ { - 1 } h _ { t } ( X , X _ { j } ^ { t e } ) .
$$

By an MCMC sampling of t from ( 180 ), one can compete ( 182 ) in each iteration. Taking average of ˆ f ( X te j | t ) over the iterations provides an estimate of the posterior mean of f on the test samples. The procedure is summarized in Algorithm 1 . In all reported experiments in Section 5 , we conduct 3000 iterations with the first 1000 iterations discarded as burn-in.

# D.2 Additional details of numerical experiments

# D.2.1 Swiss Roll data

We generate n samples X i , i = 1 ,...,n , by parametrizing X ∈ R 3 in two variables ( u,v ) as

$$
X ( u , v ) = [ u \cos ( u ) , v , u \sin ( u ) ] ,
$$

and sample u and v i.i.d. from the distribution

$$
u \sim \text {Unif} \left ( \frac { 2 \pi } { 2 } , \frac { 9 \pi } { 2 } \right ) , \ \ v \sim \text {Unif} ( 0 , 1 5 ) .
$$

[Page 79]

The true regression function f ∗ , written as a function of ( u,v ), is set to be

$$
f _ { S w i s } ( u , v ) = 4 \left ( \frac { u - 7 \pi / 2 } { 3 \pi / 2 } \right ) ^ { 2 } + \frac { \pi } { 4 5 } v .
$$

Finally, the predictor X is rescaled by

$$
X _ { 1 } \leftarrow ( X _ { 1 } + 1 5 ) / 3 0 , \ \ X _ { 2 } \leftarrow X _ { 2 } / 1 5 , \ \ X _ { 3 } \leftarrow ( X _ { 3 } + 1 5 ) / 3 0 ,
$$

so that X is lying inside [0 , 1] 3 .

# D.2.2 Mixed dimension data

The data samples { X i ,Y i } are generated according to the following procedure: With 1 / 2 probability, we randomly pick X i from the Swiss roll as defined above, and Y i generated from the same f ∗ on the 2D manifold therein. With 1 / 2 probability, we draw X i from the 1D curve

$$
X ( t ) = \frac { 7 \pi } { 2 } [ \cos ( \pi t ) \cos ( 4 \pi t ) , 1 + \cos ( \pi t ) \sin ( 4 \pi t ) , \sin ( \pi t ) ] ,
$$

where t is sampled i.i.d. from t ∼ Unif( − 1 , 1). The true regression function f ∗ on the 1D curve is set as f ∗ ( X ( t )) = ¯ f Swiss ( X ( t )), where

$$
\bar { f } _ { S w i s } ( x _ { 1 } , x _ { 2 } , x _ { 3 } ) = f _ { S w i s } ( \sqrt { x _ { 1 } ^ { 2 } + x _ { 3 } ^ { 2 } } , x _ { 2 } ) .
$$

This design ensures that the function f ∗ takes the same value on the intersection of the surface and the curve, thus preserving the continuity of the function. We rescale X to be inside [0 , 1] 3 similarly as for the Swiss Roll data.

# D.3 Additional comparison of empirical Bayes approaches

We conducted additional experiments to compare our empirical Bayes (EB) prior with alternatives on the Swiss roll data. The baselines are:

(ii’) GP estimated d : the prior is the rescaled Gamma distribution, where the manifold dimension is estimated from data as proposed in [ YD16 ]. Specifically, following [ YD16 ], we adopt the estimator of the manifold dimension as

$$
\hat { d } = \text {the closest integer to } \frac { \log 2 } { \log \hat { R } _ { k } ( X _ { 1 } ) - \log \hat { R } _ { \lceil k / 2 \rceil } ( X _ { 1 } ) } ,
$$

where ˆ R k ( x ) is the distance from x to its k -nearest neighbor in the training set { X i } n i =1 , X 1 is a random member of the training set, and k = ⌈ √ n ⌉ .

- (iv) GP max-likelihood (GP MLE): selecting the kernel bandwidth t based on maximizing the marginal likelihood. Note that this method differs from our theoretical setting because it corresponds to using a uniform prior.
- (v) GP median heuristic (GP median): setting t to be the median of the distances between samples.


We use training size up to 200 since all methods give comparable performance on larger sample sizes. The experiments are repeated for 200 runs. The results are shown in Figure A.3 . It can be seen that the median heuristic gave much larger errors, both the in-sample and out-of-sample ones. The GP MLE perform similarly as the proposed EB approach on this example; the GP with estimated d gave comparable performance on the test data, and larger in-sample error with larger variance, especially at the small training size.

To further investigate the effect of estimating dimension d , we compare the errors of (ii’) and our EB via their distributions. We choose training size 100 and increase the number of runs to 400. The distribution of the errors are plotted as histograms in Figure A.4 (b) and (d) for our EB (called “by ˆ v n ”) and (ii’) (called “by ˆ d ”) respectively. It can be seen that the out-of-sample errors of the two methods are comparable, yet

Figure A.4: Comparison of the proposed EB GP and GP with estimated dimension ˆ d . The dataset is Swiss Roll with training size 100 and testing size 2000. (a) One realization of the training set, the predictors X i are colored by the observed values Y i . (b) Histograms of the in-sample and out-of-sample errors of the proposed EB GP. The reason of these outlier errors by (ii’) is likely due to the outlier errors in estimating the dimension, that is, with a small chance the estimated ˆ d can be far from the true d (which is 2 here). This is verified by the histogram of the estimated ˆ d shown in Figure A.4 (c), which has a long tail up to 15. The training size is 100, and the Swiss Roll data at this sample size barely reveal the underlying manifold if one only considers k NN distance at a random point, see Figure A.4 (a). As a result, the dimension estimator ( 183 ) gives unstable performance at such low sample size. On this example, The error in ˆ d affects the in-sample error more visibly, possibly due to that both models are already getting large testing errors at this low training sample size. In summary, this result suggests that our EB prior based on the averaged kernel affinity statistic ˆ v n ( t ) can be more robust than EB based on manifold dimension estimation in practice, especially at relatively small sample size.
